\mathfrak{P}^{i}\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}:=\HH(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}(Q),{}^{\mathfrak{p^{\prime}}}\!\tau^{\leq i}((\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr(W)}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}},

giving an increasing filtration

\mathfrak{P}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}):=0\subset\mathfrak{P}^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}})\subset\mathfrak{P}^{2}(\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}})\cdots\subset\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}

of
\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}
. This filtration respects the algebra structure on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu}
. It is shown in
[
18
]
[Theorem 3.3] that in fact the vector space
\oplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}}\mathfrak{P}^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}})
is preserved under the Lie bracket induced by the associative algebra structure on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu}
. The BPS Lie algebra is defined to be the
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded vector subspace
10
10

10

Since we define the BPS Lie algebra after fixing the twist
\psi
, we omit it from the notation.

\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}:=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\backslash{\mathbf{0}}}\mathfrak{P}^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}})\subset\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu}.

Furthermore, by the action of tautological bundle (Section
6.3
), we have a morphism of graded vector spaces
\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu}
which induces a morphism from the tensor algebra

(12)

\displaystyle\textrm{T}_{\HH_{T}(\pt)}(\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu}.

Let
\Sym_{\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)}(\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu})\subset\textrm{T}(\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu})
be the subspace of symmetric tensors, then restricting morphism (
12
) yields a map of
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
modules

(13)

\displaystyle\Sym_{\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)}(\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu}.

We then have

Theorem 6.1

(PBW Isomorphism
[
18
]
,
[
15
]
)
.

The morphism (
13
) is an isomorphism of
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
modules. In fact, let
\Gr_{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu})
be the associated graded algebra with respect to the Perverse filtration. Then there is an isomorphism of algebras

(14)

\Gr_{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mu})\simeq\mathbf{Sym}_{\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)}(\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu})

where for any
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
module
V
,
\mathbf{Sym}_{\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)}(V)=T_{\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)}(V)/\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x=0\mid x,y\in V\rangle
is the quotient of the tensor algebra.

6.5.1.
Relative Integrality Theorem

There is a lift of Proposition
6.1
to the Relative CoHA. Following
[
15
]
, we define the BPS sheaf
\mathcal{BPS}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}
to be

(15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}:={}^{\mathfrak{p^{\prime}}}\!\tau^{\leq 1}((\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mu})_{*}(\pPhi_{\Tr(W)}))[1].

So that

(16)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}\simeq\HH(\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mu}(Q),\mathcal{BPS}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}[-1]).

By definition, we have a canonical morphism

\mathcal{BPS}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}[-1]\rightarrow\mathcal{RA}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}.

Applying the action of
\mathbb{Q}[u]
introduced in Section
6.3
, yields a morphism of complexes

\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathcal{BPS}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}[-1]\rightarrow\mathcal{RA}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}.

Since the category
\mathrm{D}^{+}(\Per^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}(Q)))
is symmetric monoidal over
[\pt/T]
, for any object
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{D}^{+}(\Per^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}(Q)))
, we may define

\Sym_{\oplus^{T}}(\mathcal{F}):=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\Sym^{i}_{\oplus^{T}}(\mathcal{F})

where
\Sym^{i}_{\oplus^{T}}(\mathcal{F})
is the
S_{i}
invariant part of

\underbrace{\mathcal{F}\boxtimes_{\oplus^{T}}\cdots\boxtimes_{\oplus^{T}}\mathcal{F}}_{i\mathrm{\ times}}.

Thus, in exactly the same way as before, since
\mathcal{RA}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}
has relative CoHA structure, the above morphism lifts to a morphism

(17)

\Sym_{\oplus^{T}}\left(\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathcal{BPS}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}[-1]\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{RA}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}

of objects in
\mathrm{D}^{+}(\Per^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}(Q)))
. We then have

Theorem 6.2

(
[
15
]
)
.

The morphism
17
is an isomorphism and for any
\mathbf{d}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\backslash 0
, there is an isomorphism of perverse sheaves

(18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}\simeq\begin{cases}\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}(W)}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)}\ &\mathrm{if}\ \mathcal{M}^{\zeta-\mathrm{st}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)\neq 0\\
0\ &\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
\Tr^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}(W):\mathcal{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the unique function through which the of morphism
\Tr^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}(W):\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
factors.

We recall the Wall Crossing theorem of Toda, which relates the BPS sheaves with or without stability.

Proposition 6.3

(
[
87
]
)
.

For any symmetric quiver
Q
, potential
W
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}}
a stability condition and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
a dimension vector, there is a natural isomorphism

(q^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\mathcal{BPS}^{\zeta}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}\simeq\mathcal{BPS}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}

where
q^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}
is the GIT affinization map

q^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}\colon\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q).

Note that the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}
lifts to a morphism of complexes in
\mathrm{D}^{\textrm{b}}(\Per(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mu}(Q)))

\displaystyle[\cdot,\cdot]\colon\mathcal{BPS}^{\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}[-1]\boxtimes_{\oplus}\mathcal{BPS}^{\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}[-1]\rightarrow\mathcal{BPS}^{\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}[-1]

which after pushforward along the map
q_{\mathbf{d}}
and applying Toda’s theorem, gives

Proposition 6.4
.

There is an isomorphism of Lie algebras

(19)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mu}\simeq\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\backslash{\mathbf{0}}}\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}.

7.
Preprojective Cohomological Hall algebra

Let
\overline{w}:\overline{Q}\rightarrow N
be a torus weighting on the doubled quiver such that the preprojective algebra relation
\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}[a,a^{*}]
is homogeneous with respect to this weighting. We then consider the
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
module
11
11

11

There also exist versions with a stability condition, however, we don’t need to consider that for our applications.

\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})

where we define
12
12

12

Note that the quotient stack
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})
is not smooth, so the defined shifted complex is not the same as the intersection complex. Note that
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}=(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})})[2(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})]
.

\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}|_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}[-2(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})]
. In
[
94
]
(For any quiver) and
[
79
]
(For the Jordan quiver), by doing virtual pullback and push forward, the authors define a
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded associative algebra structure
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}
which we refer to as the preprojective cohomological Hall algebra. We can also twist the multiplication in
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}
as we did for
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
in Section
6.4.1
. Let us denote the resulting algebra by
\mathcal{A}^{T,\chi}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}
. In a similar way, in
[
81
]
, authors define a
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded algebra structure on

\mathcal{A}^{T,\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\mathfrak{M}^{T,\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

These algebras can be seen as a particular case of the Kontsevich-Soibelman cohomological Hall algebra.

7.1.
Tripled quiver with canonical cubic potential

In Example
1
, we saw that for the tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
with canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{W}
, we have an isomorphism of algebras
\Jac(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})\simeq\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}[\omega]
; this in fact allows us to see the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}
as the dimension reduction of
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mu}
where the torus
T
action on
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})
is defined by uniquely extending the action on
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})
such that it leaves the potential
\widetilde{W}
invariant. Let

\pi:\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})

be natural forgetful morphism. We have decomposition

(20)

\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})=\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})\times\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(L)

where
L
is quiver with vertices
Q_{0}
and loops
\omega_{i}
on each vertex
i
. We may consider the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
action, which scales the loops. Then applying dimension reduction (
5.5
) gives an isomorphism of complexes

(21)

\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}\simeq\pi_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr(\widetilde{W})}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})}.

Taking cohomology, gives an isomorphism of
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
modules

(22)

\displaystyle\textrm{DR}_{\mathbf{d}}:\HH(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{T}(\widetilde{Q}),\pPhi_{\Tr(W)})\simeq\HH(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{T}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

In
[
78
]
[Appendix A] and
[
95
]
, it shown that taking sum of maps
\widetilde{\mathrm{DR}}_{\mathbf{d}}:=\mathrm{DR}_{\mathbf{d}}\cdot\binom{\mathbf{d}}{2}
over all
\mathbf{d}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}
, where
\binom{\mathbf{d}}{2}:=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\prod\binom{\mathbf{d}_{i}}{2}
gives an isomorphism of
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\zeta}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded algebras

\widetilde{\mathrm{DR}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}.

A similar analysis for the case of nilpotent representations, as done in
[
18
]
, one obtains an isomorphism of algebras

\widetilde{\mathrm{DR}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{T,\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{T,\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}.

7.2.
Support lemma

In this section, we restrict ourselves to the non-equivariant setting. We remark that these results also hold when any torus
T
. We saw that morphism
21
is an isomorphism of complexes. Combined with the Integrality Theorem
6.2
, this gives a integrality theorem for the pushforward
\JH^{\overline{Q}}_{*}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}
, where we treat
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}
as a complex on
\mathfrak{M}(\overline{Q})
. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}(\widetilde{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}(\overline{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(\overline{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{JH}^{\widetilde{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH^{\overline{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

Thus, we have an isomorphism of complexes

\displaystyle\JH^{\overline{Q}}_{*}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}\simeq\pi^{\prime}_{*}\JH^{\widetilde{Q}}_{*}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})}\simeq\Sym_{\oplus}\left(q_{*}\mathcal{BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}[-1]\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\right)

It turns out that the complex
q_{*}\mathcal{BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}[-1]
itself becomes a perverse sheaf on
\mathcal{M}(\overline{Q})
. This is explained by the support lemma. Let
l:\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})
be an embedding of stacks induced by the closed inclusion
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\Rep_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})
defined by sending a representation
\rho
of
\overline{Q}
and a scalar
\lambda
to a representation of
\widetilde{Q}
whose underlying
\overline{Q}
representation is
\rho
and the loops
\omega_{i}
act by multiplication with the scalar
\lambda
. Then it is proved in
[
19
]
[Lemma 4.1] that

Theorem 7.1

(Support Lemma)
.

There exist a unique complex

\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}\in\mathrm{D}^{+}(\Per(\mathcal{M}(\overline{Q})))

such that

\mathcal{BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq l_{*}(\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}[1]).

Thus, we have an isomorphism of complexes

(23)

\displaystyle\JH^{\overline{Q}}_{*}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}\simeq\Sym\left(\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\right)

7.3.
Kac polynomials and Graded Dimension of BPS Lie algebra

Given any quiver
Q
, for any finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and any dimension vector
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
. A representation is said to be
indecomposable
if it cannot be non-trivially written as a direct sum of two representations of
Q
. A representation
\rho
is said to be
absolutely indecomposable
if it remains indecomposable after tensoring with the closure of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, i.e.,
\rho\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is indecomposable.

Let
a_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)
be the number of isomorphism classes of absolutely indecomposable
\mathbf{d}
dimensional representations of
Q
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. There is an intricate relationship between the Kac polynomials and the Lie algebra associated with the quiver, which can be considered as the generalization of Gabriel’s Theorem, which classifies finite type quivers. We recall

Definition 7.2

(Associated Kac-Moody Lie algebra)
.

For any quiver
Q
, the real subquiver
Q^{\prime}
is defined to be the subquiver of
Q
containing those vertices of
Q
that do not support any edge loops, along with all arrows between these vertices.
Let
\mathfrak{g}_{Q^{\prime}}
be the associated Kac-Moody Lie algebra. It is defined as a Lie algebra generated by symbols
e_{i}.f_{i},h_{i};i\in Q^{\prime}_{0}
with the relations

\displaystyle[h_{i},e_{j}]=a_{ij}e_{j}

\displaystyle[h_{i},f_{j}]=-a_{ij}f_{j}

\displaystyle[e_{i},f_{j}]=\delta_{ij}h_{i}

\displaystyle[e_{i},\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt]^{a_{ij}+1}(e_{j})=0;

\displaystyle[f_{i},\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt]^{a_{ij}+1}(f_{j})=0;

where
a_{ij}
is the number of edges joining
i
and
j
in the graph obtained from
Q^{\prime}
. We identify
\mathbb{Z}^{Q^{\prime}_{0}}
along with the symmetrized Euler form
\chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\textbf{e})+\chi_{Q}(\textbf{e},\mathbf{d})
with the root lattice
\mathrm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{g}_{Q^{\prime}}}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q^{\prime}}
along with the Cartan pairing via the morphism
\mathbf{d}\mapsto\sum\mathbf{d}_{i}\alpha_{i}
where
\alpha_{i}
are the simple roots of
\mathfrak{g}_{Q^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{\prime}}
be the Lie subalgebra generated by
e_{i}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{Q^{\prime}}
denote the Lie subalgebra generated by
f_{i}
.

Then it is a Theorem of Kac that:

Theorem 7.3

(
[
38
]
)
.

a_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)
is a monic polynomial in
q
of degree
1-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}
. It doesn’t depend on the orientation of
Q
. For a quiver without loops,
a_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)
is non-zero if and only if
\mathbf{d}
is a positive root of the associated Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
.

Example 3

(Finite type)
.

If
Q
if finite type
ADE
quiver then by theorem of Gabriel,

(24)

\displaystyle a_{Q^{ADE},\mathbf{d}}(q)=\begin{cases}1,\mathrm{when\ }(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})=1(\mathbf{d}\ \mathrm{\ is\ a\ positive\ root})\\
0,\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

More generally, for an arbitrary quiver
Q
, there exists a formula to compute the Kac Polynomial due to Hua
[
35
]
. Kac conjectured that the coefficients of
a_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(Q)
are always positive. For indivisible dimensional vectors, this was proved in 2004 by Crawley-Boevey and Van den Bergh (
[
11
]
). Then, using DT invariants, Mozgovoy proved this for a quiver without loops in
[
62
]
. Finally, this conjecture was settled by Hausel, Letellier,
and Rodriguez-Villegas in
[
34
]
for arbitrary quivers. There is also a proof of this conjecture using BPS Lie algebras. In fact, in
[
20
]
, Davison proved this conjecture by realizing the coefficients of Kac polynomials as the graded dimensions of BPS Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
. We have

Theorem 7.4

(
[
19
]
)
.

For any quiver
Q
,

(25)

\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\dim(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,i}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}})q^{i/2}=a_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q^{-1}).

Example 4
.

We can also calculate the size of the Nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra. Let
i:\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{N_{\overline{Q}}}}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})
be the inclusion of the coarse moduli space of nilpotent
\overline{Q}
representations, clearly
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{N_{\overline{Q}}}}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})
is projective. We have a Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{JH}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{JH}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

and so we have

\displaystyle\JH_{*}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}\simeq i^{!}\JH^{\overline{Q}}_{*}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})}\simeq\ \Sym\left(i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\right)

and thus there is an isomorphism of graded vector spaces

\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\Sym\left(\HH(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q}),i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}})\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\right)

But since Verdier duality functor
\mathbb{D}
commutes with the vanishing cycles and intersection cohomology sheaf, it follows that

\displaystyle\HH(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q}),i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}})\simeq(\HH_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q}),i^{*}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}))^{\vee}

Note that
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q})
is homotopic to
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q})
via torus
T
action which scales the arrows, for which the BPS sheaf is equivariant. Thus we have

\displaystyle\HH_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q}),i^{*}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}})

\displaystyle=\HH(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q}),i^{*}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}})\simeq\HH(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\overline{Q}),i^{*}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}})\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}.

Thus we have isomorphism of graded vector spaaces

\HH(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q}),i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}})\simeq(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}})^{\vee}

and so we recover

(26)

\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\dim(\HH^{i}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{N}_{\overline{Q}}}(\overline{Q}),i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}))q^{i/2}=a_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q).

The above relations allow us to conclude non-trivial statements about the BPS Lie algebra. For any quiver
Q
, since
\Tr(\widetilde{W})=0
for
\mathbf{d}=\delta_{i}
, we have an isomorphism of vector spaces
\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\delta_{i}}\simeq\mathbb{C}[x_{i,1}]
. We define
\alpha_{i}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}^{\chi}
to be the inverse of
1\in\mathbb{C}[x_{i,1}]
. Clearly
\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\delta_{i}}
when the vertex
i
doesn’t support any edge loops. We claim that for any
i,j
,
a_{Q,\delta_{j}+(1+a_{ij})\delta_{i}}(q)=0
, this is because Kac’s polynomials are orientation independent. So if we choose the orientation such that all the arrows are from vertex
i
to
j
, then there is no indecomposable representation of dimension
\delta_{j}+(1+a_{ij})\delta_{i}
. We thus have a map of Lie algebras

(27)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{\prime}}^{+}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

given by
e_{i}\mapsto\alpha_{i}
where
\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{+}
. The equality mentioned above suffices to show that this morphism is an injection. This is done by comparing the action on the Nakajima quiver varieties (See Section
13.1
) with Nakajima’s action, which is known to be faithful. We have

Proposition 7.5

(
[
22
]
, Theorem 6.6)
.

The map (
27
) gives an isomorphism of Lie algebras
13
13

13

In
[
22
]
, one writes the above isomorphism for the negative half. But to our perspective, it is more natural to identify it to the positive half as the cohomological Hall algebra acts by
creation
operators on the cohomology of Nakajima quiver varieties.

\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{\prime}}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}.

7.4.
Flat deformation

In the case when the natural mixed Hodge structure on
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}
is pure, the deformed cohomological Hall algebra
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
forms a flat deformation of
\mathcal{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
\mathbf{w}\colon Q_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{s}
be a
W
invariant weighting function and let
v\colon\mathbb{Z}^{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{s^{\prime}}
be a surjective morphism inducing an inclusion of tori
T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
where
T^{\prime}
is the torus associated to weighting
v\mathbf{w}
. Choose a splitting of
v:\mathbb{Z}^{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{s^{\prime}}
inducing an isomorphism
\HH_{T}(\pt)\simeq\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)\otimes\HH_{T^{\prime\prime}}(\pt).

Assume the mixed Hodge structure on
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}
is pure. Then we have an isomorphism of
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
modules

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\simeq

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime}}_{Q,W}\otimes\HH_{T^{\prime\prime}}(\pt)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T}_{Q,W}\simeq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{Q,W}\otimes\HH_{T^{\prime\prime}}(\pt),

while there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)
algebras

\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\HH_{T}(\pt)}\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)\simeq\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\psi}_{Q,W}

and
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)
Lie algebras

\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\HH_{T}(\pt)}\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{Q,W}.

Proof.

This statement is proved for
\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}
in
[
19
]
[Theorem 9.6]. The proof works as it is in this case, as it only relies on the purity of
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}
. The statement
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T}_{Q,W}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{Q,W}\otimes\HH_{T^{\prime\prime}}(\pt)
as vector spaces follows from the PBW theorem (Proposition
6.1
) while the isomorphism of algebras
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\HH_{T}(\pt)}\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)\simeq\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\psi}_{Q,W}
implies in particular that there is map of
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded Lie algebras

\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\HH_{T}(\pt)}\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{Q,W}
, which then becomes an isomorphism by a size argument.
∎

We will, in particular, be applying the above proposition in the setting of the tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
with tripled canonical potential
\widetilde{W}
, which is shown to be of pure mixed Hodge structure in
[
19
]
[Theorem A]. In particular, this implies that

Theorem 7.7
.

The cohomological Hall algebera
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is free module over
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
.

A similar theorem holds for the case of nilpotent CoHA. It is proved in
[
19
]
and
[
80
]
that

Theorem 7.8
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
modules

\mathcal{A}^{T,\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
i.e. The nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra
\mathcal{A}^{T,\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is free module over
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{T}}(\pt)
.

8.
BPS Lie algebra of Cyclic quivers

Let
Q^{K}=\widetilde{A_{K}}
be the cyclic quiver of length
K+1
, i.e. we have
K+1
vertices
0,1,\cdots,K
with arrows
i\rightarrow i+1
for
i=0,\cdots,K
and an arrow
K\rightarrow 0
. We then consider the tripled cyclic quiver
\widetilde{Q^{K}}
with the canonical potential
\widetilde{W^{K}}
as in Example
1
(See Figure
1
). Let
T
be any torus action which leaves the potential
\widetilde{W_{K}}
invariant. We consider two distinct choices for the torus
T
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{30}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{30}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{23}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{01}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{12}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{2}}

Figure 1.
\widetilde{Q^{3}},\widetilde{W^{3}}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}}\omega_{i}(a_{i,i+1}^{*}a_{i,i+1}-a_{i,i-1}a_{i,i-1}^{*})

•

Action of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
given by weighting function
\textbf{w}:\widetilde{Q^{K}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, such that
\textbf{w}(a)=1
,
\textbf{w}(a^{*})=-1
and
\textbf{w}(\omega_{i})=0
for all vertices
i\in Q^{K}_{0}
.

•

Action of
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
given by weighting function
\textbf{w}:\widetilde{Q^{K}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}
where
\textbf{w}(a)=(1,0)
,
\textbf{w}(a^{*})=(0,1)
and
\textbf{w}(\omega_{i})=(-1,-1)
for all vertices
i\in Q^{K}_{0}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
, the cohomological Hall algebra of the tripled cyclic quiver with canonical potential and its
\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
\mathbb{T}
deformed versions by
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}^{*},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{T}^{*},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
respectively.

8.0.1.
(Deformed) BPS Lie algebra of Cyclic quiver

In this section, we shall calculate the BPS Lie algebra for tripled cyclic quivers with a canonical cubic potential. Following the work of Davison, Hennecart, and Schelegel Mejia, these Lie algebras are now known for arbitrary tripled quivers with canonical potential (See Section
8.1
).

In the case of cyclic quivers, we can calculate these Lie algebras more directly, as we explain next. We first calculate the Kac polynomial for cyclic quivers, which tells us about the graded dimensions of BPS Lie algebras. One can check that (See
[
36
]
[Proposition 5.0.1])

Proposition 8.1
.

The Kac polynomial of the cyclic quiver
Q^{K}
is given by

(28)

a_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)=\begin{cases}&q+K\textrm{ when }\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cdot\delta\textrm{ (positive imaginary root)}\\
&1\textrm{ when }\mathbf{d}\in\mathrm{\Delta}^{+}_{\textrm{re}}\textrm{ (positive real root)}\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{\Delta}^{+}_{\textrm{re}}
and
\delta=(1,\cdots,1)
are respectively, the real positive roots and the primitive imaginary root of the affine Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{sl}}_{K+1}
.

Proposition 8.2
.

There is an isomorphism of Lie algebras

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}\oplus s\mathbb{Q}[s]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,\mathbb{C}^{*}}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}\oplus s\mathbb{Q}[s])\otimes\HH_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\pt)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,\mathbb{T}}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}\oplus s\mathbb{Q}[s])\otimes\HH_{\mathbb{T}}(\pt)

where
\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{K}}\oplus s\mathbb{Q}[s]
is a trivial central extension of
\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}
and
\deg(s^{n})=n\cdot\delta
.

Proof.

From Proposition
7.5
, we have an isomorphism of Lie algebras

\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}\simeq\HH^{0}(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}).

In particular this also shows that
\HH^{0}(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}})
is generated as a Lie algebra by
\alpha_{i}
, where we recall that
\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta_{i}}
. The Kac polynomial of the cyclic quiver (
8.1
) and it’s relation with BPS Lie algebra (
25
) also tells us that for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, there exist
\gamma_{n\cdot\delta}\in\HH^{-2}(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},n\cdot\delta})
. By equation (
8.1
), there is no element of cohomological degree
-4
in dimension
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\delta
. Thus it follows that
[\gamma_{n\cdot\delta},\gamma_{m\cdot\delta}]=0
. Again, by equation
8.1
, there is no
-2
cohomological degree term in dimension
\delta+\delta_{i}
for any
i
, thus it follows that
[\gamma_{n\cdot\delta},\alpha_{i}]=0
. But then
\alpha_{i}
generates
\HH^{0}(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}})
and all of
\HH^{-2}(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}})
is central and so we are done. This argument does not work for deformed versions. However in
[
14
]
[Corollary 11.9], it is shown that for any quiver
Q
and for any torus action such that the canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{W}
remains invariant,
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is a trivial deformation of
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
, i.e. there is an isomorphism of Lie algebras

\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\HH_{T}(\pt).

Hence, the conclusion follows.
∎

The BPS Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
for tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
and potential
\widetilde{W}
are now completely known in terms of generators and relations. This also gives a candidate for a double of the BPS Lie algebra. We explain the construction in general.

8.1.
Generalized Kac-Moody Lie algebra

In this section, we will recall the construction of the Generalized Kac-Moody Lie algebra for a monoid with a weight function defined in
[
14
]
, based on the earlier construction of Borcherds-Bozec Kac-Moody Lie algebras in
[
8
]
.

Let
(M,+)
be a monoid. Let
(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt):M\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the symmetric bilinear form. Then we define

Definition 8.3

(Primitive Positive Roots)
.

The set of primitive positive roots
\sum_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}
is defined to be the set of
m\in M,m\neq 0
such that
2-(m,m)\geq 0
and
2-(m,m)>\sum_{j=1}^{s}(2-(m_{j},m_{j}))
for any non-trivial decomposition
m=\sum_{j=1}^{s}m_{j}
with
m_{j}\in M,m_{j}\neq 0
.

Definition 8.4

(Simple Positive Roots)
.

The set of simple positive roots
\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}
is defined to be the union of the set of primitive positive roots
\sum_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}
and
lm,l\geq 1
where
m\in\sum_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}
and
(m,m)=0
.

Then define subsets

\displaystyle\Phi^{+,\mathrm{real}}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}

\displaystyle:=\{m\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}\mid(m,m)=2\}\mathrm{\ (\ Real\ positive\ roots\ )}

\displaystyle\Phi^{+,\mathrm{im}}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}

\displaystyle:=\{m\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}\mid(m,m)\leq 0\}\mathrm{\ (\ Imaginary\ positive\ roots\ )}

\displaystyle\Phi^{+,\mathrm{iso}}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}

\displaystyle:=\{m\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}\mid(m,m)=0\}\mathrm{\ (\ Isotopic\ positive\ roots\ )}

\displaystyle\Phi^{+,\mathrm{hyp}}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}

\displaystyle:=\{m\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}\mid(m,m)<0\}\mathrm{\ (\ Hyperbolic\ positive\ roots\ )}

Definition 8.5

(Cartan Matrix)
.

Let

A_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}=(a_{m,n}=(m,n))_{m,n\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}}

to be the Cartan Matrix for
M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)
.

The final piece of the data is the weight function.

Definition 8.6

(Weight Function)
.

Let

P\colon\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}[t^{\pm 1/2}]

given by
P\mapsto P_{m}(t^{1/2})
be the weight function. We assume that
P_{m}(t^{1/2})=1
whenever
(m,m)=2
.

Let
P_{m}(t^{1/2})=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}p_{m,j}t^{j/2}
with
p_{m,j}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 8.7

(Generalized Kac Moody Lie algebra for Monoid and Weight Function)
.

Given a monoid
M
with symmetric bilinar form
(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)
, such that
(m,n)\leq 0
if
m\neq n
and
(m,m)\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 1}
. The generalized Kac Moody Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt),P}
is defined to be the Lie algebra generated by
e_{m,j,l},f_{m,j,l},h_{m}
with
m\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)},j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\leq l\leq p_{m,j}
, with the relations

\displaystyle h_{m+n}

\displaystyle=h_{m}+h_{n}

\displaystyle[h_{m},h_{n}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[h_{m},e_{n,j,l}]

\displaystyle=a_{m,n}e_{n,j,l}

\displaystyle[h_{m},f_{n,j,l}]

\displaystyle=-a_{m,n}f_{n,j,l}

\displaystyle[e_{m,j,l},f_{n,j^{\prime},l^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,n}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{l,l^{\prime}}h_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}(e_{m,j,l})^{1-a_{m,n}}(e_{n,j^{\prime},l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=0\mathrm{\ if\ }a_{m,n}=2

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}(f_{m,j,l})^{1-a_{m,n}}(f_{n,j^{\prime},l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=0\mathrm{\ if\ }a_{m,n}=2

\displaystyle[e_{m,j,l},e_{n,j^{\prime},l^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=[f_{m,j,l},f_{n,j^{\prime},l^{\prime}}]=0\mathrm{\ if\ }a_{m,n}=0

We place
e_{m,j,l}
in cohomological degree
j
, each
f_{m,j,l}
in cohomological degree
-j
and
h_{m}
of cohomological degee
0
. We define positive half
\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt),P}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt),P}
by Lie subalgebra generated by
e_{m,j,l}
. Similarly define Cartan
\mathfrak{h}_{M,(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt),P}
as the Lie subalgebra generated by
h_{m}
. We then have the following theorem.

Theorem 8.8

(
[
14
]
)
.

Let
Q
be any quiver. Let
M=\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
and let
(\mathbf{d},\textbf{e})_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}=\chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\textbf{e})+\chi_{Q}(\textbf{e},\mathbf{d})
be the bilinear form. Let

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{d})=\begin{cases}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dim\HH^{j}(\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})})t^{j/2}\mathrm{\ when\ }\mathbf{d}\in\Phi^{+,\mathrm{real}}_{\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}},(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}}\backslash\Phi^{+,\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}},(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}}\\
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dim\HH^{j}(\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})})t^{j/2}\mathrm{\ when\ }\mathbf{d}=l\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\Phi^{+,\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}},(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\sum^{+}_{\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}},(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}}\end{cases}

be the weight function. Then there is an isomorphism of Lie algebras:

\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}},(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}},P}.

We will refer to the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}},(\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt,\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt)_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}},P}
by
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q}
.

Example 5

(Cyclic Quivers)
.

Let
Q
be the cyclic quiver
Q^{K}
. Then the Symmetrized euler form is given by

2\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})^{2}.\right)

So only solutions to
2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}\geq 0
is given by strings of type
(n,\cdots,n,n+1,n+1,\cdots,n+1,n,\cdots,n)
, i.e
\mathbf{d}=n\cdot\delta+[i,m)
or
(n+1)\cdot\delta
for some
n\geq 0
,
i\in[0,K]
and
0\leq m\leq K-1
(See section
1
for Notation).

Moreover if
\mathbf{d}=n\cdot\delta+[i,m)
, then it cannot be a primitive root since one can write
2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})=0
, but
2-(n\cdot\delta,n\cdot\delta)_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}}}=2
. Similarly if
\mathbf{d}=[i,m)=\delta_{i}+\delta_{i+1}+\cdots+\delta_{i+m-1}
then also it can’t be a primitive root as then
2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}}}=0
but then so does
2-(\delta_{i},\delta_{i})_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}}}
which contradicts the primitiveness of
\mathbf{d}
. Thus, we have real positive roots
\delta_{i}
for
i\in[0,K]
and imaginary positive roots
l\delta,l\geq 1
. Now we consider the weight function.

\displaystyle P(\delta_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum\dim(\HH^{j}(\mathbb{Q}_{\pt}))t^{j/2}=1

\displaystyle P(l\delta)

\displaystyle=\sum\dim(\HH^{j}(\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}})}))t^{j/2}=t^{-1}

So we have generators
e_{i},f_{i},h_{i}
for
i\in[0,K]
of cohomological degree
0
and
e_{l\delta},f_{l\delta},h_{l\delta}
of cohomological degree
-2,0,2
respectively satisfying the relations above, i.e the affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{gl}}(K+1)
.

We can also define the notion of a Verma Module.

Definition 8.9

(Verma Module of Generalized Kac Moody Lie algebra )
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM,\leq 0}_{Q}
be the negative Borel, i.e., Lie subalgebra generated by
f_{m,j,l}
and
h_{m}
. For
\mathbf{f}\in\Hom(\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z})
, we define the Verma module as

V_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q},\mathbf{f}}=\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q})\otimes_{\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM,\leq 0}_{Q})}\mathbb{C}

where
\mathbb{C}
becomes a
\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM,\leq 0}_{Q})
module via the quotient
\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM,\leq 0}_{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{h}_{M})
and
h_{m}\cdot 1=\mathbf{f}(m)
.

Then, analogous to the usual case of semisimple Lie algebras, one defines

Definition 8.10
.

The Lowest Weight module
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q},\mathbf{f}}
is defined to be quotient of
V_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q},\mathbf{f}}
by the unique maximal submodule of
V_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q},\mathbf{f}}
which doesn’t contain
1\otimes 1
.

9.
Affinized BPS Lie Algebra and Factorization Coproduct

For any symmetric quiver
Q
and potential
W
, we saw in the Section
6.5
, that there exists a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{Q,W}
such that
\Sym(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{Q,W}[u])\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
as vector spaces. This makes it natural to wonder if the vector subspace
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{Q,W}[u]\subset\mathcal{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
is itself closed under the commutator Lie bracket coming from the associative algebra structure on
\mathcal{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
. The answer to this question is no for a general quiver with potential
Q,W
14
14

14

This can be seen when
Q,W
comes from the derived category of resolved conifold and will be explained in future work.
. However, the answer becomes yes in the case of the tripled quiver with potential
\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}
. This is obtained by constructing a coproduct which uses factorization structure on the stack of representations of the Jacobi algebra for the tripled quiver with potential and is outlined by Davison in
[
18
]
. This is proved rigorously in author’s PhD thesis
[
36
]
[Chapter 6] and will be used subsequently in this paper. The construction for more general 3-Calabi–Yau completions
of 2-Calabi–Yau categories will appear in the joint work of Davison, Hennecart, Kinjo, Schiffmann and Vasserot.

In the case of tripled quiver with canonical potential, there is an isomorphism of algebras
\Jac(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})\simeq\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}[\omega]
and thus a representation of
\Jac(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})
is the same as a representation of preprojective algebra
\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}
with an endomorphism. This allows us to define

Definition 9.1
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be any open ball. Then we define
\mathfrak{M}^{U}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})\subset\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})
to be open substack of representations such that generalized eigenvalues of
\omega_{i}
are all inside
U
. a

Using the support lemma of the BPS sheaves
7.1
, it is proved in
[
36
]
[Proposition 6.1.3, 6.1.4] that there is an isomorphism

\HH(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q}),\pPhi_{\Tr(W)_{\mathbf{d}}})\simeq\HH(\mathfrak{M}^{U}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q}),\pPhi_{\Tr(W)_{\mathbf{d}}|_{U}}).

Furthermore, for two disjoint open balls
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
, since any point
\rho\in\mathfrak{M}^{U_{1}\coprod U_{2}}(\Jac(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}))
admits a canonical direct sum decompotion
\rho_{1}\oplus\rho_{2}
such that generalized eigenvalues of
\omega
acting on
\rho_{1}
are in in
U_{1}
and of
\omega
acting on
\rho_{2}
are in
U_{2}
, we have an isomorphism of stacks

(29)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{U_{1}\coprod U_{2}}(\Jac(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}))\simeq\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{U_{1}}(\Jac(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}))\times\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{U_{2}}(\Jac(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})).

Then using Thom-Sebastiani isomoprhism and deformed dimensional reduction, it is proved in
[
36
]
[Proposition 6.1.8] that we have isomorphism

(30)

\displaystyle\HH(\mathfrak{M}^{U_{1}\coprod U_{2}}(\widetilde{Q}),\pPhi_{\Tr(W)|_{U_{1}\coprod U_{2}}})\simeq\HH(\mathfrak{M}^{U_{1}}(\widetilde{Q}),\pPhi_{\Tr(W)|_{U_{1}}})\otimes\HH(\mathfrak{M}^{U_{2}}(\widetilde{Q}),\pPhi_{\Tr(W)|_{U_{2}}}).

Combining (
29
) and (
30
), we get a morphism

\mathrm{\Delta}:\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}.

It is proved in
[
36
]
[Proposition 6.1.13] that this morphism is compatible with the CoHA structure. Furthermore, it is co-commutative
[
36
]
[Proposition 6.1.12], since
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
can be exchanged via a homotopy. Thus by the Milnor-Moore theorem
\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\bU(\mathcal{P})
where
\mathcal{P}
are the primitive elements with respect to the coproduct. Furthermore, it is proved in
[
36
]
[Proposition 6.1.15] that
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[u]
is primitive under this coproduct and by the PBW theorem (Proposition
6.1
),
\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\Sym(\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[u])
and so by considering the size of
\mathcal{P}
, it follows that
\mathcal{P}=\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[u]
. This shows that the sub vector space
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[u]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is closed under the Lie bracket.

Definition 9.2

(The affinized BPS Lie algebra)
.

For any quiver
Q
, the affinized BPS Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is defined to be the Lie algebra structure on the
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded vector space

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}:=\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[u]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{Q,W}

where the Lie bracket is defined by taking the commutator Lie bracket coming from the associative algebra structure on
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
.

Convention 2
.

Given an element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
, we denote by

\alpha^{(n)}:=u^{n}\cdot\alpha\in\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

Let
\textbf{w}:\widetilde{Q}\rightarrow N
be a torus weighting such that
\textbf{w}(\omega_{i})=0
for all
i\in Q_{0}
15
15

15

This condition is cruicial. This construction doesn’t work, for example, in the case of tripled ADE quiver with canonical potential with torus weighting
\textbf{w}(a)=1,\textbf{w}(a^{*})=1
and
\textbf{w}(\omega_{i})=-2
for all
i\in Q_{0}
.
. Let
T^{\prime}=\Hom(N,\mathbb{C}^{*})
. Then since
\textbf{w}(\omega)=0
, where
\omega=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\omega_{i}
, the morphism

\lambda\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})\rightarrow\Sym(\mathbb{A}^{1})

which records the generalized eigenvalue, is
T^{\prime}
equivariant. This induces a morphism

\lambda^{T}\colon\mathfrak{M}^{T^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{d}}(\widetilde{Q})\rightarrow\Sym(\mathbb{A}^{1}).

We can apply the construction of factorization coproduct to
\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
, where we consider
\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
as an object in the category of
\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)
modules, i.e. we have a
\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)
module morphism

\mathrm{\Delta}^{T}\colon\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes_{\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)}\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}.

Considering the primitive elements of the cocommutative coproduct
\mathrm{\Delta}^{T}
, shows that the
\HH_{T}(\pt)
module

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}:=\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[u]\subset\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

has a structure of Lie algebra, where the Lie bracket is defined by taking the commutator Lie bracket, coming from the associative algebra structure on
\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
. Furthermore, there is an isomorphism of
\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)
algebras

\bU_{\HH_{T^{\prime}}(\pt)}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}})\simeq\mathcal{A}^{T^{\prime},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}.

We call
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,T^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
the
T^{\prime}\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt
deformed affinized BPS Lie algebra. We calculate
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
and it’s deformation, for the case of the cylic quivers in Section
16
and
19
respectively.

10.
Heis Lie algebra action

The action of the tautological bundle on the vector space
\mathcal{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
increases the cohomological degree by
2
. We can describe the other half of this action, which acts by decreasing the cohomological degree. This structure has been exploited in
[
37
]
as a translation operator in the construction of vertex algebras and by
[
22
]
for the calculation of the affinized BPS Lie algebra for the Jordan quiver. We consider the action by scaling by the automorphisms of
\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)
. More precisely, we have a morphism

\displaystyle\act:\BCu\times\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)

defined by sending the tautological line bundle
\mathcal{L}
and family of representations
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{F\otimes L}
. Doing the pullback (
5.2
), gives a map of sheaves

(31)

\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)})\rightarrow\act_{*}(\mathbb{Q}_{\BCu}\boxtimes\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss},\psi}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)})).

After shifting and taking global sections, this gives a map of cohomologically graded vector spaces

\act^{*}:\HH(\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q),\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})})\rightarrow\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\HH(\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q),\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}).

In particular, for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}
we have

\act^{*}(\alpha)=\sum_{n\geq 0}u^{n}\otimes\alpha_{(n)}.

We define

\partial\colon\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}

by
\alpha\mapsto\alpha_{(1)}
. Since the morphism
\act^{*}
preserves the cohomological degree,
\partial
is an operator which decreases the cohomological degree by
-2
. We then have

Proposition 10.1
.

[
18
]

The morphisms

\displaystyle\partial:\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}

\displaystyle u:\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}

define derivations on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}
i.e.

\displaystyle\partial(a\star b)

\displaystyle=(\partial a)\star b+a\star(\partial b)

\displaystyle u(a\star b)

\displaystyle=(u\cdot a)\star b+a\star(u\cdot b).

Furthermore, for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}
, we have
[\partial,u]\alpha=|\mathbf{d}|\alpha
.

We now show that the operators
\partial
and
u
interact with the perverse filtration
\mathfrak{P}
on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta,\psi}_{Q,W}
in a nice way.

Proposition 10.2
.

The action
u
increases the perverse degree by
2
while
\partial
decreases the perverse degree by
2
, i.e.

\displaystyle u\cdot\mathfrak{P}^{i}

\displaystyle\subset\mathfrak{P}^{i+2}

\displaystyle\partial\cdot\mathfrak{P}^{i}

\displaystyle\subset\mathfrak{P}^{i-2}

Proof.

Applying
\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}
to morphism (
31
), gives a morphism of complexes in
\mathrm{D}^{\textrm{b}}(\mathcal{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q))

a:(\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)})\rightarrow(\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\act_{*}(\mathbb{Q}_{\BCu}\boxtimes\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)})).

After taking semisimplification, the action map collapses, and so we have the following commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\BCu\times\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH_{\mathbf{d}}^{T,\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\act}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}}}

where
\pi_{2}
is the projection to the second component. Thus we have

(\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\act_{*}(\mathbb{Q}_{\BCu}\boxtimes\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)}))\simeq\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes(\JH^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)}).

But since

\tau^{\leq n}(\HH^{2k}(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes(\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}(Q)}))=\tau^{\leq n-2k}((\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}})),

the morphism
\tau^{\leq n}(a)
factors through

\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\HH^{2k}(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\tau^{n-2k}((\JH^{T,\zeta}_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr(W_{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{T,\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d}}})).

So for any
\alpha\in\mathfrak{P}^{i}
,
\act^{*}(\alpha)=\sum_{n\geq 0}u^{n}\alpha_{(n)}
where
\alpha_{n}\in\mathfrak{P}^{i-2n}
and thus in particular
\alpha_{1}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\leq i-2}
. Finally, the action of
u
increases the perverse degree, which is exactly
[
15
]
[Lemma 5.8] applied to the determinant bundle.

∎

The above argument also shows that if
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}
then
\partial(\alpha_{\mathbf{d}})=0
, since the perverse filtration starts from degree
1
(Section
6.5
). Now, by repetitively applying the Proposition
10.2
, it follows that

Proposition 10.3
.

Let
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,T,\zeta}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}
, then
u^{n}\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{P}^{2n+1}
and we have
\partial(u^{n}\alpha_{\mathbf{d}})=n|\mathbf{d}|u^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}.

The action of
u,\partial
can be interpreted as an action of the 3-dimensional Heisenberg
\mathbf{Heis}
on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\zeta}_{Q,W}
, as considered in
[
18
]
.

Definition 10.4
.

Let
Heis
be the Lie algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
having basis
p,q,c
such that

[q,p]=c,

c
is central. We give
Heis
a ‘cohomological’ grading by setting elements
p,q,c
as elements of cohomological degree
2,-2,0
respectively. Then
M
is said to be a graded
Heis
module, if
M=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}M_{2i}
and the
\mathbf{Heis}
action respects the grading, i.e.
p(M_{2i})\subset M_{2i+2},q(M_{2i})\subset M_{2i-2},c(M_{2i})\subset M_{2i}
.

If a graded
\mathbf{Heis}
module
\mathfrak{L}
has a Lie algebra structure, then it is said to be a
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra if
p
and
q
act by Lie algebra derivations on
\mathfrak{L}
. In many cases, a much smaller subspace controls
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebras. For any
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{L}
, let
\mathfrak{L}^{\leq 0}=\oplus_{i\leq 0}\mathfrak{L}_{2i}
be the the subspace of non-positive even graded elements. Clearly
\mathfrak{L}^{\leq 0}\subset\mathfrak{L}
forms a Lie subalgebra.

Definition 10.5

(Negatively determined
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebras)
.

We say that a
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{L}
is negatively determined if
\mathfrak{L}_{i}=0
for all odd
i
and the map
q:\mathfrak{L}_{2i}\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}_{2i-2}
is an isomorphism of vector spaces for any
i>0
.

If we have two negatively determined Lie algebras, then a morphism between them is also negatively determined.

Proposition 10.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{H}
be two negatively determined
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebras such that there is a homomorphism of Lie algebras

\phi^{\leq 0}\colon\mathfrak{L}^{\leq 0}\rightarrow\mathfrak{H}^{\leq 0}

which commutes with the action of
\mathbf{Heis}
, i.e, where for any
\alpha_{-2i}\in\mathfrak{L}_{-2i},i>0
,
\phi^{\leq 0}(p\alpha_{-2i})=p\phi^{\leq 0}(\alpha_{-2i})
, for any
\alpha_{-2i+2}\in\mathfrak{L}_{-2i+2},i>0
,
\phi^{\leq 0}(q\alpha_{-2i+2})=q\phi^{\leq 0}(\alpha_{-2i+2})
and for any
\alpha_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}_{0},\phi^{\leq 0}(c\alpha_{0})=c\phi^{\leq 0}(\alpha_{0})
. Then, the map
\phi^{\leq 0}
extends uniquely to map
\phi\colon\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow\mathfrak{H}
of graded
\mathbf{Heis}
modules.
Furthermore,
\phi
is a Lie algebra homomorphism if and only if
\phi([\alpha_{2i},\phi_{2j}])=[\phi(\alpha_{2i}),\phi(\alpha_{2j})]
for
i+j\leq 0
.

Proof.

For any element
\alpha_{2i}\in\mathfrak{L}_{2i}
with
i>0
, we define
\phi(\alpha_{2i})
to be the unique element in
\mathfrak{H}_{2i}
such that
q^{i}\phi(\alpha_{2i})=\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i}\alpha_{2i})
and for any
\alpha_{2i}\in\mathfrak{L}_{2i}
with
i\leq 0
, we set
\phi(\alpha_{2i})=\phi^{\leq 0}(\alpha_{2i})
. The map is well defined by the injectivity of
q
on positive components.

The morphism
\phi
is a map of
\mathbf{Heis}
modules since when
i=1
, any
\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}_{2}
satisfies
q\phi(\alpha_{2})=\phi^{\leq 0}(q\alpha_{2})
and if
i>1
, then
\phi(q\alpha_{2i})
is defined to be unique element in
\mathfrak{H}
such that
q^{i-1}\phi(q\alpha_{2i})=\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i}\alpha_{2i})
, which is clearly satisfied by
q\phi(\alpha_{2i})
. Similarly, since
q^{i}c\phi(\alpha_{2i})=cq^{i}\phi(\alpha_{2i})=c\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i}\alpha_{2i})=\phi^{\leq 0}(cq^{i}\alpha_{2i})=\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i}c\alpha_{2i})
implies that
\phi(c\alpha_{2i})=c\phi(\alpha_{2i})
. Finally succesive application of
[q,p]=c
implies that for any
\alpha\in\mathfrak{H}
or
\mathfrak{L}
,
q^{i+1}p\alpha=pq^{i+1}\alpha+(i+1)q^{i}c\alpha
. So
q^{i+1}(p\phi(\alpha_{2i}))=(pq^{i+1}+(i+1)q^{i}c)\phi(\alpha_{2i})=pq\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i}\alpha_{2i})+(i+1)c\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i}\alpha_{2i})=\phi^{\leq 0}((pq^{i+1}+(i+1)cq^{i})\alpha_{2i})=\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i+1}p\alpha_{2i})
. Thus
p\phi(\alpha_{2i})=\phi(p\alpha_{2i})
.

It suffices to check that it is indeed a Lie algebra homomorphism if
\phi([\alpha_{2i},\phi_{2j}])=[\phi(\alpha_{2i}),\phi(\alpha_{2j})]
for
i+j\leq 0
. For this, we consider
\alpha_{2i}\in\mathfrak{L}_{2i}
and
\beta_{2i}\in\mathfrak{L}_{2j}
. If
i+j\leq 0
, then
\phi([\alpha_{2i},\alpha_{2j}])=([\phi(\alpha_{2i}),\phi(\alpha_{2j})])
by the assumption. So, we can assume
i+j>0
. Then

\phi([\alpha_{2i},\alpha_{2j}])
is the unique element such that

q^{i+j}\phi([\alpha_{2i},\alpha_{2j}])=\phi^{\leq 0}(q^{i+j}[\alpha_{2i},\beta_{2j}])=\sum_{r=0}^{i+j}\binom{i+j}{r}\phi^{\leq 0}[q^{r}\alpha_{2i},q^{i+j-r}\alpha_{2j}]

By assumption, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{i+j}\binom{i+j}{r}\phi^{\leq 0}[q^{r}\alpha_{2i},q^{i+j-r}\alpha_{2j}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{i+j}\binom{i+j}{r}[\phi(q^{r}\alpha_{2i}),\phi(q^{i+j-r}\alpha_{2j})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{i+j}\binom{i+j}{r}[q^{r}\phi(\alpha_{2i}),q^{i+j-r}\phi(\alpha_{2j})]

\displaystyle=q^{i+j}[\phi(\alpha_{2i}),\phi(\beta_{2j})].

and thus
\phi([\alpha_{2i},\alpha_{2j}])=[\phi(\alpha_{2i}),\phi(\alpha_{2j})]
for all
i,j
and we are done.
∎

Definition 10.7
.

We say that a
\mathbf{Heis}
module
M
is integrable if it admits a
\mathbf{Heis}
module decomposition
M=\oplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}M^{[n]}
such that each the central element
c
acts on
M^{[n]}
by scaling with
n
. We correspondingly say that
M
is integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra if it it integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
module,
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra, and if the Lie algebra structure is also
\mathbb{Z}
graded (i.e
[M^{[n]},M^{[m]}]\subset M^{[n+m]}
).

An example of an integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra is as follows.

Definition 10.8
.

For any Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
we define
\mathfrak{g}[D]:=\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}[D]
to be a Lie algebra given by basis
\alpha D^{n}
where
n\geq 0
and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}
with the relations
[\alpha D^{n},\alpha^{{}^{\prime}}D^{m}]=[\alpha,\alpha^{{}^{\prime}}]D^{n+m}
where
\alpha,\alpha^{{}^{\prime}}\in\mathfrak{g}
and
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Proposition 10.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
graded Lie algebra. Then

\mathfrak{g}[D]=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{m}[D]

is negatively determined integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra defined by
q(\alpha_{m}D^{n})=n\alpha_{m}D^{n-1}
,
c(\alpha_{m}D^{n})=m\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}D^{n}
and
p(\alpha_{m}D^{n})=m\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}D^{n+1}
for any
\alpha_{m}\in\mathfrak{g}_{m}
.

We then have the following consequence of Proposition
10.6
.

Proposition 10.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a negatively determined integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra such that
\mathfrak{L}_{i}=0
for all
i<0
. Then there is an isomorphism of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{L}_{0}[D]\simeq\mathfrak{L}
where
\mathfrak{L}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{L}
is the degree
0
Lie subalgebra.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{L}
is integral, it admits a decomposition
\mathfrak{L}=\oplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{L}^{[m]}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{0}=\oplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{L}^{[m]}_{0}
is
\mathbb{Z}
graded Lie algebra. By Proposition
10.9
, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{L}_{0}[D]
is integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebras. Note that
\mathfrak{L}_{0}[D]
and
\mathfrak{L}
are both negative determined Lie algebras. Thus by Proposition
10.6
, the morphism
\id:\mathfrak{L}_{0}\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}_{0}
extends to a morphism of Lie algebras
\phi:\mathfrak{L}_{0}[D]\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}
. This morphism is an injection, since for a non zero
\alpha\in\mathfrak{L}_{0}
,
q^{i}(\phi(\alpha D^{i}))=\alpha\neq 0.
Similarly it is surjection since for any
\alpha_{2i}\in\mathfrak{L}_{2i}
,
\phi((q^{i}\alpha_{2i})D^{i})=\alpha_{2i}
.
∎

Next, we see that affinized BPS Lie algebras are also negatively determined integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebras.

Proposition 10.11
.

For any quiver
Q
, the affinized BPS Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is negatively determined integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra.

Proof.

From Proposition
10.2
, it follows that

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z},\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,i}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z},\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}},|\mathbf{d}|=m}\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,i}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}\right)

is integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra. The cohomology degrees of the BPS Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is determined by the Kac polymomial (
7.3
) and in particular, it implies that
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,i}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}=0
if
i>0
and for all odd integers
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. But then since there is an isomorphism of cohomologically graded vector spaces
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}[u]
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is spanned by
u^{n}\cdot\alpha
where
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
and in particular if the cohomological degree of
u^{n}\cdot\alpha>0
then
n\geq 1
. For a fixed dimension vector
\mathbf{d}
, the derivation
\partial
is a morphism of vector spaces
\partial^{i}_{\mathbf{d}}\colon\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,i}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,i-2}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}
. When
i
is an even positive integer then by Proposition
10.3
, the map
\partial^{i}_{\mathbf{d}}
is surjective since it sends the elements
u^{n}\alpha
for
n>0
to
n|\mathbf{d}|u^{n-1}\alpha
. But by above argument or Equation
25
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,i}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,i-2}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}
has the same dimension when
i>0
and thus
\partial
is in fact isomorphism and we are done.
∎

11.
Spherical subalgebra of CoHA

We define the spherical part of a
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
graded algebra as a subalgebra generated by the smallest possible dimension vectors.

Definition 11.1

(Spherical Generation)
.

For any quiver
Q
, let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
graded algebra over a ring
R
. Then we say that
\mathcal{A}
is spherically generated if
A
is generated as an
R
algebra by
\langle\mathcal{A}_{\delta_{i}}\mid i\in Q_{0}\rangle
. The spherical part of algebra
\mathcal{A}
is the subalgebra generated by
\langle\mathcal{A}_{\delta_{i}}\mid i\in Q_{0}\rangle
and is denoted by
\mathcal{SA}
.

We then have the following interesting consequence of the above abstraction. We determine
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
for any quiver without loops.

Theorem 11.2
.

For any quiver
Q
, let
Q^{\prime}
be the subquiver formed after removing all the loops of
Q
. Then

(1)

The subvector space
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is closed under the Lie bracket and we have a Lie algebra isomorphism

\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\simeq\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{\prime}}^{+}[D].

(2)

For any
Q
, there is an embedding of algebras

\bU(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{\prime}}[D])\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

which is an isomorphism if and only if the quiver
Q
is of finite type.

(3)

For quiver
Q
without loops, the image of above embedding is the spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
, so that we have an isomorphism of algebras

\bU(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{\prime}}[D])\simeq\mathcal{S}\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}.

Proof.

For part (1), we note that given
\alpha^{(n)}=u^{n}\cdot\alpha
and
\beta^{(m)}:=u^{m}\cdot\beta
in
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})
where
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
, we have

(32)

[\alpha^{(n)},\beta^{(m)}]=\sum_{i\geq 0}u^{n+m+i}\gamma_{i}

for some
\gamma_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,-2i}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
, since
[\alpha^{(n)},\beta^{(m)}]
is of cohomological degree
2n+2m
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is all supported in non-positive cohomological degrees (see proof of Proposition
10.11
). We claim that almost all the terms in the above summation are superfluous, i.e., we claim that
\gamma_{i}=0
for all
i>0
. Since the summation in (
32
) is a finite sum, let
N>0
be the smallest such that
\gamma_{i}=0
for all
i>N
.
We apply the derivation
\partial
,
n+m+N
times to the Equation
32
and using Proposition
10.3
gives that

0=(n+m+N)!(|\mathbf{d}|)^{n+m+N}\gamma_{N}

where
\mathbf{d}
is the dimension vector on which
[\alpha^{(n)},\beta^{(m)}]
are supported. Thus
\gamma_{N}=0
. Contradicting the minimality of
N
. Thus
N=0
and the claim follows.

Thus
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
is closed under the Lie bracket. It is a negatively determined integrable
\mathbf{Heis}
Lie algebra which starts from degree
0
. Thus by Proposition
10.10
, there is an isomoprhism of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q})\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}[D]
. Further, by the Proposition
7.5
, there is an isomorphism of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{\prime}}
and so we are done. Part (2) follows from the fact that Kac polynomials are constant if and only if
Q
is of finite type. Finally for part (3), we observe that the subalgebra generated by
\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\delta_{i}}
is exactly the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie subalgebra of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
generated by
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}\delta_{i}}
for
i\in Q_{0}
. But then for a quiver without loops, we have
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\delta_{i}}=\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\delta_{i}}
and so we are done by the above argument.

∎

12.
Nakajima Quiver Varieties for Cyclic Quivers

In this chapter, we will consider the action of cohomological Hall algebras on the cohomology of Nakajima quiver varieties. These actions have been defined in
[
94
]
,
[
22
]
,
[
81
]
in the case of stability condition
\zeta^{-}
, which demands that the image of the framed vector generates the entire representation (Section
14.1.2
). When the quiver is cyclic, this gives an action of CoHA on the cohomology of the equivariant Hilbert Scheme. However, we can do similar consturction for a different stability condition (Section
14.9
)
\zeta^{n}
which allows us to define action of a subalgebra of CoHA on the cohomology of Hilbert scheme of points on the resolution of Kleinian singularity
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{K}
(Section
14.14
). We study these two actions by comparing them with the work of Nakajima, where they defined the action of Kac-Moody Lie algebra on the cohomology of the equivariant Hilbert scheme and the action of Heisenberg Lie algebra on the cohomology of the Hilbert scheme of points.

Given any quiver
Q
, fix an identification of the vertex set
Q_{0}
with the set
[0,1,\dots,K]
where
K=|Q_{0}|-1
. Let
\mathbf{f}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
be a dimension vector, called the framing vector. Then we consider the space of representations:

\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q):=\prod_{a\in Q_{1}}\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}(s(a))},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}(t(a))})\times\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{f}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}}).

Then the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\Rep_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
carries the action of
\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}
which preserves the symplectic form. So we have the moment map

(33)

\mu_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}:T^{*}\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{\mathbf{d}}.

Then Nakajima quiver variety is defined by taking the GIT quotient of
\mu_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}^{-1}(0)
. By a trick due to Crawley-Boevey
[
12
]
, we can look at the space
T^{*}\Rep_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
as a representation of another quiver
Q_{\mathbf{f}}
of dimension
(\mathbf{d},1)
.
Q_{\mathbf{f}}
is defined by adding a vertex
\infty
to the vertex set
Q_{0}
, and an arrows
\alpha_{i,n}
for all
i\in Q_{0},1\leq n\leq\mathbf{f}_{i}
where the source
s(\alpha_{i,n})=\infty
and
t(\alpha_{i,n})=i
. For any dimension vector
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
and number
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(\mathbf{d},n)\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}
be the dimension vector where we denote by
(\mathbf{d},n)_{\infty}=n
and
(\mathbf{d},n)_{i}=\mathbf{d}_{i}
for all
i\in Q_{0}
. Here to get a representation of quiver
Q_{\mathbf{f}}
from
\Rep_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
, we are essentially identifiying
\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{f}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}})
with
\Hom(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}})^{\mathbf{f}_{i}}
.

Note that the moment map for
(\mathbf{d},1)
dimensional representation of the preprojective algebra gives a morphism

\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}:\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}

The morphism
33
is the composition of
\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}
and the projection
\mathfrak{gl}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{\mathbf{d}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}\colon\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{gl}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{gl}_{\mathbf{d}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

So we have an extra relation

\sum_{i\in Q_{0},1\leq n\leq\mathbf{f}_{i}}\rho(\alpha_{i,n}^{*})\rho(\alpha_{i,n})=0

in
\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}^{-1}(0)
.
However, since the dimension of the vertex
\infty
is
1
, this is same condition as having

(34)

\sum_{i\in Q_{0},1\leq n\leq\mathbf{f}_{i}}\Tr(\rho(\alpha_{i,n}^{*})\rho(\alpha_{i,n}))=0.

However this is superflous since the defining relations for
\mu_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})}^{-1}(0)
are

\sum_{a\in Q_{0}}[\rho(a),\rho(a^{*})]+\sum_{i\in Q_{0},1\leq n\leq\mathbf{f}_{i}}\rho(\alpha_{i,n})\rho(\alpha_{i,n}^{*})=0

but then
\Tr([A,B])=0
so this implies that

\sum_{i\in Q_{0},1\leq n\leq\mathbf{f}_{i}}\Tr(\rho(\alpha_{i,n})\rho(\alpha_{i,n}^{*}))=0

which is same as equation
34
. Thus there are essentially no new equations and so

\mu_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}^{-1}(0)=\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}^{-1}(0).

So from now on we will working with later point of view. Taking the affine GIT quotient defines

Definition 12.1

(Affine Nakajima Quiver Variety)
.

The affine Nakajima quiver variety is defined to be the affine quotient

\mathrm{N}^{0}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)=\mathbb{C}[\mu^{-1}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}(0)]^{\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}}].

The varieties
\mathrm{N}^{0}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
are singular. There is a way to define an open subset of
\mu^{-1}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}(0)
where the action of
\GL_{\mathbf{d}}
is free. Given a dimension vector
\mathbf{d}
and a stability condition
\zeta\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}}
, we extend this to a stability condition
\hat{\zeta}\in\mathbb{Q}^{(Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{0}}
by setting
\zeta_{\infty}=-\sum\mathbf{d}_{i}\zeta_{i}
. Assume that
\hat{\zeta}
is generic for dimension vector
(\mathbf{d},1)
. This defines an open subset
\mu^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}\subset\mu^{-1}_{{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}}(0)
of
\hat{\zeta}
stable representations.

Example 6

(
\zeta^{\infty}
stability condition)
.

For any quiver
Q
, let
\zeta\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}}
be any tuple where
\zeta_{i}<0
for all
i\in Q_{0}
. Then
\hat{\zeta}
is generic for any dimension
(\mathbf{d},1)
since
\chi_{\hat{\zeta}}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},0)=\sum\zeta_{i}\mathbf{d}_{i}\neq 0
for all
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},0)<(\mathbf{d},1)
unless
\mathbf{d}_{i}=0
and
\chi_{\hat{\zeta}}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},1)=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\zeta_{i}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{i})
unless all
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},1)=(\mathbf{d},1)
. Infact since
\chi_{\hat{\zeta}}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},1)
is always positive unless
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}
implies that for any
\zeta
stable representation of quiver
Q
, it cannot be stable unless
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}
. Also,
\chi_{\hat{\zeta}}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},0)
is always negative, so it doesn’t impose any condition on the stability. Thus, a representation of
\zeta
is semistable iff it is generated by the image of a vector at the vertex
\infty
. We shall denote this stability condition by
\zeta^{\infty}
.

Definition 12.2

(Nakajima quiver variety)
.

Given quiver
Q
, dimension vector
\mathbf{d}
, framing vector
\mathbf{f}
and generic stability condition
\zeta
, the Nakajima quiver variety is defined to be the quotient

\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)=\mu^{-1}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}(0)^{\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}

We have the following fundamental theorem of Nakajima.

Theorem 12.3
.

The open subset
\mu^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-\mathrm{ss}}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}
is a non-singular complex variety of dimension
\dim(\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(Q_{\mathbf{f}}))-\dim(\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}})=\sum_{a\in Q_{0}}(\mathbf{d}_{s(a)}\mathbf{d}_{t(a)})+2\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathbf{d}_{i}\mathbf{f}_{i}-\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathbf{d}_{i}^{2}
and the Nakajima quiver variety
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
is a non-singular complex variety of dimension
\dim(\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(Q_{\mathbf{f}}))-2\dim(\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}})=2\sum_{a\in Q_{0}}(\mathbf{d}_{s(a)}\mathbf{d}_{t(a)})+2\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathbf{d}_{i}\mathbf{f}_{i}-2\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathbf{d}_{i}^{2}=-2\chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})+2\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{f}
.

Note that for any weighting
\mathbf{w}:(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{r}
for which
\sum_{a\in Q_{\mathbf{f}}}[a,a^{*}]
is homogenous. Then the corresponding
T
action preserves
\mu^{-1}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}(0)
and thus defines an action on Nakajima quiver variety.
By the definition of GIT, there exists a projective morphism

(35)

q:\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathrm{N}^{0}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)

which is a symplectic resolution whenever surjective.

By definition, Nakajima quiver varieties come with non-trivial tautological bundles for every vertex:

Definition 12.4

(Tautological Bundles on Nakajima quiver Variety)
.

For each vertex
i
of the quiver, we define tautological bundle
\mathcal{V}_{i}
to be the vector bundle associated with the
\GL_{d_{i}}
principal bundle

\displaystyle(\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}^{-1}(0)\cap\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}))/\GL^{i}_{\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)

where by
\GL^{i}_{\mathbf{d}}
we mean
\prod_{j\neq i}\GL_{d_{j}}
, and the action on
\mu_{(\mathbf{d},1)}^{-1}(0)\cap\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})/\GL^{i}_{\mathbf{d}}
is given by the injection
\prod_{j\neq i}\GL_{d_{j}}\rightarrow\GL_{\mathbf{d}}
.

Later, we will also consider topologically trivial vector bundles
\mathcal{W}_{i}
of rank
\mathbf{f}_{i}
defined by
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{f}_{i}}\rightarrow\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
. Nakajima quiver variety carries an action of the torus
T
, and the equivariant Chern class of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
will then carry non-trivial information.

In Section
4.1
, we defined the Lusztig nilpotent stack of preprojective algebra representations. Analogously, we can define

Definition 12.5
.

Given any quiver
Q
, dimnesion
\mathbf{d}
, framing
\mathbf{f}
and stability condition
\zeta
, we define nilpotent quiver variety
\mathrm{L}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
as the closed subvariety
q^{-1}(0)
where
q:\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathrm{N}^{0}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
is the GIT projective morphism defined in equation
35
.

Note that since
q
is a projective morphism,
\mathrm{L}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
is always projective.

12.1.
Critical locus description of Nakajima quiver varieties

In
[
22
]
, Davison provides a critical locus description of the Nakajima quiver varieties
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{<}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
. Their method doesn’t work for an arbitrary generic stability condition
\zeta
. In this section, using dimension reduction, we will show that the statement is true at the level of cohomology.

Given framing
\mathbf{f}
, we consider the tripled framed quiver
\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}}
. This is the usual doubled framed quiver
\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}
that we used to define the Nakajima quiver
varieties, but with an extra loop at every vertex, including a loop at the framing vertex. We then consider the canonical cubic potential

\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}}=\left(\sum_{i\in(Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{0}}\omega_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{a\in(Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{1}}[a,a^{*}]\right).

Assume that
\hat{\zeta}
is generic for dimension vector
(\mathbf{d},1)
