. Then the 3d Nakajima quiver variety is defined to be smooth variety

\mathcal{M}^{\zeta,3d}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q):=\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}})/\GL_{\mathbf{d}},

where
\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}})\subset\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}})
the open subset of
\zeta
semistable representations. We can similarly define
\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})\subset\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})
the open subset of
\hat{\zeta}
stable
\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}
representations. Let
\Tr^{\zeta}(\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}})
denote the restriction of the trace function. We then have

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
\pi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}:\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}})\rightarrow\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})
be the projection map. Then

\crit(\Tr^{\zeta}(\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}}))\cap\pi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}))=\crit(\Tr^{\zeta}(\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}}))\cap\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1)}^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}})

Proof.

This proof uses same argument as in
[
19
]
[Lemma 6.3]. Let
\rho\in\crit(\Tr^{\zeta}(\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}}))\cap\Rep_{(\mathbf{d},1}^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}})\backslash\crit(\Tr^{\zeta}(\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}}))\cap\pi_{Q_{\fr},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}))
. Then since
\Jac(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}},\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}})\simeq\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}[\omega]
, we have that
\rho=(\rho^{\prime},f)
where
\rho^{\prime}
is a representation of
\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\fr}}
with an endomorphism
f\in\End_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}}(\rho^{\prime})
. Our assumtion on
\rho
is equivalent to
\rho^{\prime}
being not a semistable representation of
\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}
. But then by the existence of Harder-Narasimhan filtrations, there exists a filtration of
\rho^{\prime}
as a
\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}
module

0=\rho_{0}^{\prime}\subset\rho_{1}^{\prime}\subset\rho_{2}^{\prime}\cdots\subset\rho_{n}=\rho^{\prime},

such that the slopes
\mu(\rho_{i}^{\prime}/\rho_{i-1}^{\prime})
are strictly decreasing. Each of
\rho_{i}^{\prime}/\rho_{i-1}^{\prime}
is
\hat{\zeta}
semistable. Consider the restriction
f_{|\rho_{1}^{\prime}}:\rho_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow\rho_{n}^{\prime}
. Since
\mu(\rho_{1}^{\prime})>\mu(\rho_{n}^{\prime}/\rho_{n-1}^{\prime})
,
f_{|\rho_{1}^{\prime}}\colon\rho_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow\rho_{n}^{\prime}\rightarrow\rho_{n}^{\prime}/\rho_{n-1}^{\prime}
is the
0
morphism. Thus
f_{|\rho_{1}^{\prime}}
factors through
\rho_{n-1}^{\prime}
. Since
\mu(\rho_{1}^{\prime})>\mu(\rho_{i}^{\prime}/\rho_{i-1}^{\prime})
for all
i
, we may continue this argument inductively to conclude that
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
is invariant under
f
. Thus
(\rho_{1}^{\prime},f_{|\rho_{0}^{\prime}})
forms a sub representation of
\rho
with higher slope, contradicting the semistability of
\rho
.
∎

Note that

\pi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}))=\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})\times\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{\mathbf{f}}}\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}})

is a
\GL_{\mathbf{d}}
equivariant decomposition. Thus, by the above lemma and the dimensional reduction theorem (
5.5
) applied in the case when

\displaystyle\overline{X}

\displaystyle=\pi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}))

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\Rep^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d},1)}(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{n}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{\mathbf{f}}}\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}}),

it follows that

\mathrm{DR}:\textrm{H}^{T}_{c}(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta,3d}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta}(\widetilde{W}_{\mathbf{f}})}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)}[2(\mathbf{d},1)\cdot(\mathbf{d},1)])\simeq\textrm{H}^{T}_{c}(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\mathbb{Q}).

Thus, by applying Verdier duality, since
\dim(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta,3d}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)-\dim(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})-2(\sum_{i\in Q_{\mathbf{f}}}\mathbf{d}_{i}^{2})=-1
, it follows that

Proposition 12.7
.

Let
\zeta\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}}
by any stability condition such that
\hat{\zeta}
is generic for dimension vector
(\mathbf{d},1)
. Then we have a natural isomorphism of graded vector spaces

(36)

(-1)^{\binom{\mathbf{d}}{2}}\mathrm{DR}:\HH^{T}(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta,3d}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta}(\widetilde{W_{\mathbf{f}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta,3d}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)}[-1])\simeq\HH^{T}(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

Note that here we have twisted dimension reduction isomorphism by the sign
(-)^{\binom{\mathbf{d}}{2}}
, this is so that we have compatibility with the action of preprojective algebra, as we shall later see.

13.
CoHA Action

In this section, we will construct the action of COHA on the Nakajima quiver variety for the stability condition
\zeta^{\infty}
. This is done in
[
18
]
in the formulation we explain next and in
[
93
]
,
[
81
]
for preprojective CoHA’s. We will also see the compatibility with the cup product of the tautological line bundle. Later, we will see how these ideas can be used to define an action of a subalgebra of CoHA for a different stability condition.

Given two dimension vectors
\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{2}
and framing
\mathbf{f}
, let

\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{\infty},3d}_{(\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{2},\mathbf{f})}(Q)=\{0\rightarrow(\rho_{1})_{(\mathbf{d}_{1},0)}\rightarrow(\rho_{2})^{\zeta^{\infty}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(\mathbf{d}_{1}+\mathbf{d}_{2},1)}\rightarrow(\rho_{3})_{(\mathbf{d}_{2},1)}\rightarrow 0\}

denote the stack of short exact sequences where
\rho_{2}
is
\zeta^{\infty}
semistable. We then have the usual correspondence diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}_{1}}(\widetilde{Q})\times\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{\infty},3d}_{\mathbf{d}_{2},\mathbf{f}}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{\infty},3d}_{(\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{2},\mathbf{f})}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}_{1}+\mathbf{d}_{2},\mathbf{f}}^{\zeta^{\infty},3d}(Q)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

where the morphism
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3}
is projection to
(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})
and
\pi_{2}
is projection to
\rho_{2}
.
\pi_{2}
is a proper map, since the
\zeta^{\infty}
stability condition implies that every
(\mathbf{d}_{2},1)
dimensional quotient of a
(\mathbf{d}_{1}+\mathbf{d}_{2},1)
dimensional
\zeta^{\infty}
semistable representation is also semistable. Then, by doing push-pull along vanishing cycle cohomology, it follows that we have a left action of
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
on

\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HH(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{\infty},3d}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\pPhi_{\Tr(\widetilde{W_{\mathbf{f}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{\infty}-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)}[-1])\simeq\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})=:\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)

Here, the second isomorphism is defined in Proposition
12.7
.

Proposition 13.1
.

For any quiver
Q
and framing
\mathbf{f}
, the cohomological Hall algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}^{\chi}
acts on the cohomology of Nakajima quiver variety
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
.

The proof that the above defines an action is the same for associativity of cohomological Hall algebra as proved in
[
48
]
. We denote this action by
\bullet
.

Since we have defined the action of CoHA, there is an action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
. In theorem
8.8
, we saw that
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
can be realized as a positive Half of the Generalized Kac Moody Lie algebra. Infact the action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
can be lifted to action of full
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q}
.

Theorem 13.2

(
[
14
]
, Theorem 10.5)
.

The
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
action on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
extends to
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q}
action and there is decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q}
modules

\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)\simeq\bigoplus_{(\mathbf{d},1)\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}}}\HH^{*}(\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}},(\mathbf{d},1)}))\otimes L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q},\hat{\mathbf{d}}}.

where
\hat{\mathbf{d}}\in\Hom(\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}},\mathbb{Z})
given by
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\mapsto(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}-\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
.

Example 7
.

(\mathbf{0},1)
is always a real positive root. Thus
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q},\hat{\mathbf{0}}}
where
\hat{\mathbf{0}}:\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
is always a summand in
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
.

On the other hand, there is the action of preprojective CoHA
\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
defined in
[
94
]
[Section 5] and
[
81
]
[Section 5.6]. However, it is shown in
[
95
]
[Theorem A] that these actions are compatible under the twisted dimension reduction morphism
\widetilde{\mathrm{DR}}
in Section
7.1
.

Although we will not be pursuing this until section
18
, the perspective we want to give is that
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{\overline{N}}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}
should be thought of as the opposite half of the CoHA in the following way. First, it is shown in
[
81
]
that we can lift the above action to the case of nilpotent quiver variety(One can also prove using the above construction).

Proposition 13.3

(
[
82
]
)
.

For any quiver
Q
and framing
\mathbf{f}
, the nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{N},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
acts on the cohomology of nilpotent Nakajima quiver variety
16
16

16

Note that the nilpotent quiver variety
\Lus^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
aren’t always smooth.

\mathrm{L}^{\zeta^{\infty},\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q):=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HH^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Lus^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\mathbb{Q}[2(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})-2\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{d}]).

Now, we claim that there is duality

\HH(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\simeq\HH^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Lus^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\mathbb{Q}[2(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})-2\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{d}])^{*}

This is simply because the varieties
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
and
\Lus^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
are homotopic. But
\Lus^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q)
is also a projective variety. Hence, its usual cohomology is the same as the compactly supported cohomology, which is Poincaré dual to the Borel-Moore homology. Thus by Proposition
13.3
, we get a
right
action of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{N}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
.

Remark 13.4
.

Note that there is addition action of
\GL_{\mathbf{f}}
on
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{\infty},3d}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}}(Q)
for any quiver
Q
which reparametrize the framing of
\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbf{f}}
. Then the action of CoHA also works equivariantly. We get a left action of
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
on

\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HH^{T\times\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{f}}}(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}}(Q),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

and similarly a right action of
\mathcal{A}^{T,\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
. In the case when
T
scales the symplectic form non-trivially, due to a theorem of
[
6
]
and
[
82
]
, these CoHA can be realized as positive and negative half of MO Yangian respectively, which implies that after taking direct sum across all framings, these actions are faithful(See Chapter
18
). However, faithfulness is conjectural when
T
acts trivially on the symplectic form.

13.0.1.
Compatibility with Cup product

Note that, in the same way, we constructed the action of tautological bundles on
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}
in Section
6.3
, we can construct analogus action of the tautological line bundle
\otimes_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathcal{V}_{i}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
by
u\cdot v:=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\cup v
. The following proposition will be useful later to understand the induced action of the affinized
\BPS
Lie algebra.

Proposition 13.5
.

The action of
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
on
\mathrm{\mathrm{N}}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)
satisfies

(u\cdot\alpha)\bullet v=u\cdot(\alpha\bullet v)-\alpha\bullet(u\cdot v)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle u\cdot(\alpha\bullet v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\cup((\pi_{2})_{*}(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3})^{*}(\alpha\otimes v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}(\pi_{2})_{*}((\pi_{2})^{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i}))\cup(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3})^{*}(\alpha\otimes v))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}(\pi_{2})_{*}((\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3})^{*}((c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\otimes 1+1\otimes c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i}))\cdot(\alpha\otimes v))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}(u\cdot\alpha)\bullet v+\alpha\bullet(u\cdot v)

where the third equality is true since for a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow L_{1}\rightarrow L_{2}\rightarrow L_{3}\rightarrow 0
,
c_{1}(L_{2})=c_{1}(L_{1})+c_{1}(L_{3})
.
∎

14.
Kleinian singularity and McKay correspondence

Let
G\subset\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be a finite subgroup. The McKay correspondence associates to every finite subgroup of
\textrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
a Dynkin graph of affine type ADE. Let
\rho_{0},\rho_{1},\cdots,\rho_{d-1}
be the set of all the isomorphism classes of irreducible representations of the group
G
where
\rho_{0}
is assumed to be the trivial representation. Since
G\subset\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\subset\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, we have the tautological representation of
G
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Let
a_{ij}
be the multiplicities in the decomposition of the tensor product
\rho_{i}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
into the sum of irreducible representations, that is

\rho_{i}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\simeq\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}\rho_{j}^{a_{ij}}.

Then the graph with the set of vertices
0,1,\cdots,d-1
and
a_{ij}
edges between the vertices
i
and
j
is the corresponding McKay graph. We now consider the finite cylic group of order
K+1

\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}=\langle\omega\rangle\subset\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
embedded in
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
by
\omega\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\omega&0\\
0&\omega^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
where
\omega
is a primitive
K+1
-th root of unity. The McKay graph corresponding to the group
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
gives the doubled cyclic quiver
\overline{Q^{K}}
.

On the other hand, we may consider the affine quotient
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
. It is a singular space with a singularity at the origin. Interestingly, the Nakajima quiver variety associated with the quiver cyclic quiver
Q^{K}
gives a minimal resolution of this singularity. In the case we are interested in, these varieties can be understood as equivariant Hilbert schemes.

14.1.
Equivariant Hilbert scheme

Let
\Hilb(\mathbb{C}^{2})
denote the Hilbert scheme of points on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, we have an induced action of
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
on
\Hilb(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. For any finite group
G
, let
R(G)
be the monoid of the isomorphism classes of finite-dimensional representations of the group
G
. For every representation
V\in R(\mathbb{Z}_{K+1})
, let

\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})=\{J\subset\mathbb{C}[x,y],\mathbb{C}[x,y]/J\simeq_{\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}}V\}

where
\simeq_{\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}}
denote isomorphism as representations of the finite group
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
. This gives a decomposition into components

(37)

\displaystyle(\Hilb(\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}}=\bigoplus_{V\in R(\mathbb{Z}_{K+1})}\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2}).

where
(\Hilb(\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}}
is the fixed point locus of the
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
action on
\Hilb(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Note that since
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
is abelian, all the irreducible representations are
1

1
-dimensional. Thus for every representation
V
,
V\simeq\oplus\rho_{i}^{\oplus\mathbf{d}_{i}}
as
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
representations, where
\rho_{i}
is the
1
dimensional representation of weight
i
, given by
v\mapsto\omega^{i}v
. This gives an isomorphism of monoids
\mathbf{d}:R(\mathbb{Z}_{K+1})\mapsto\mathbb{N}^{K+1}
.

Example 8
.

Consider the ideal
I
of the functions which vanish at the points of a free
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
-orbit. The orbit consists of
K+1
elements and the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
-module
\mathbb{C}[x,y]/I
is a regular representation
\rho_{\textrm{reg}}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
. In fact
\mathbf{d}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})=\underbrace{(1,1,\cdots,1)}_{\text{$K+1$ times}}=\delta
. The action of
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is free outside the origin. The restriction of the Hilbert-Chow map (Proposition
14.2
) gives the minimal resolution
\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
.

14.1.1.
Tutological bundles

By the way we have defined them,
\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
is an
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
invariant subvariety of
\Hilb^{\dim(V)}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Denote by
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{n}
, the tautological rank
\dim(V)
bundle on the Hilbert scheme
\Hilb^{\dim(V)}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
(See Definition
14.6
). Consider the restriction
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{\dim(V)})|_{\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})}
over
\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Then, each fibre carries a structure of representation of
G
. We thus have

(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{n})_{\mid\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})}\simeq\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}}\mathcal{H}_{k}\otimes\rho_{k},

giving tautological bundles
\mathcal{H}_{k}
on
\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Note that we can equivalently write
\mathcal{H}_{k}=\Hom(\rho_{i},(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{n})_{\mid\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})})

14.1.2.
Nakajima quiver variety description

The equivariant Hilbert scheme is an example of a Nakajima quiver variety.

Proposition 14.1

(
[
68
]
)
.

We have an isomorphism of smooth connected algebraic varieties

\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\simeq\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d}(V),\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})

and so their dimension is
2\mathbf{d}_{0}(V)-\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}}(\mathbf{d}_{i}(V)-\mathbf{d}_{i+1}(V))^{2}
.

On closed points, given an ideal
J
, the above isomorphism sends
J
to a representation of
\overline{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}
, given by

V_{i}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}}(\rho_{i},\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2}]/J)

for
i\in[0,K]
and
V_{\infty}=\mathbb{C}
and the morphisms between these vector spaces are given by composition with the morphisms
z_{1}:\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2}]/J\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2}]/J
and
z_{2}:\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2}]/J\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2}]/J
given by multiplication by
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
respectively. In particular, this means that the above isomorphism sends the tautological bundles
\mathcal{H}_{i}
on
\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
to the tautological bundles
\mathcal{V}_{i}
on the Nakajima quiver variety
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\mathbf{d}(V),\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
.

Convention 3
.

Given any smooth variety
X
and an irreducible closed subvariety
V
, let

[V]\in\HH^{2(\dim(X)-\dim(V))}(X)

be the fundamental class
[
2
]
[Appendix A].

14.1.3.

on

\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})\simeq\bigoplus_{V\in R(\mathbb{Z}_{K+1})}\text{H}(\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})

due to
[
65
]
17
17

17

There is an action for general Nakajima quiver variety for quiver without loops. Here we only focus on a special case.
. For each
i
and

, the Hecke correspondence is defined to be the subvariety

\mathfrak{B}_{i}(V):=\{(I_{1},I_{2})\in\Hilb^{V-\rho_{i}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\times\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2})|I_{2}\subset I_{1}\}\subset\Hilb^{V-\rho_{i}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\times\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2}).

Note that
x,y
act trivially on
I_{1}/I_{2}
. By

]
[Section 5],
\mathfrak{B}_{i}(V)
are known to be smooth Lagrangian submanifolds inside the product

Let

be the standard Chevalley generators of

as in (

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}_{i}(V)}}

\displaystyle e_{i}(v)

\displaystyle:=(-1)^{d_{i}(V-\rho_{i})+d_{i+1}(V-\rho_{i})}(p_{2})_{*}(p_{1}^{*}(v)\cup[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(V)]).

Strictly speaking, in the original work, Nakajima constructed operators on a Lagrangian subvariety of

14.1.4.
Minimal resolution as Nakajima quiver variety

Theorem 14.2

(
[
49
]
)
.

The affine Nakajima quiver variety
\mathrm{N}^{0}_{\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
is isomorphic to the affine quotient
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
and the quiver variety
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q)\simeq\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
is the minimal resolution via the GIT map
18
18

18

There is more general result for any finite subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

\pi\colon\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{(\delta,\delta_{0})}(Q^{K})\rightarrow\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{0}}_{(\delta,\delta_{0})}(Q^{K})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}.

The exceptional fibre
\pi^{-1}(0)
components can be understood as Nakajima correspondences defined in the previous section. Consider
\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})
for
i\neq 0
. Then by Proposition
14.1
, the dimension of
\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}-\rho_{i}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
is
0
and thus
\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}-\rho_{i}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})=\{\pt_{i}\}
, for some point
\pt_{i}\in\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}-\rho_{i}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Thus by definition, we may view
\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})
as a subvariety of
\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
:

(39)

\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})=\{\pt_{i}\subset I\}\subset\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}}\simeq S_{K}

The subvarieties
\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})
are smooth Lagrangian manifold inside the surface
S_{K}
. These are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}

[
67
]
[Proposition 6.2]. In
[
67
]
[Example 6.3], it is shown that the exceptional fiber
\pi^{-1}(0)
is the union of
\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})
. For any representation
\rho
of
\overline{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}
of dimension
(\mathbf{d},1)
and for any arrow
a\in Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}
, let
\rho_{a}:\mathbb{C}^{s(a)}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{t(a)}
be the corresponding linear map and
\rho^{*}_{a}:\mathbb{C}^{t(a)}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{s(a)}
be the map in the opposite direction.

Example 9
.

We can observe that
\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})
correspond to the isomorphism classes of representations of dimension
\delta
where
\rho_{a}=1,\rho_{a}^{*}=0
where
a:j-1\rightarrow j
for
j=1,\cdots,i-1
;
\rho_{a}=0,\rho_{a}^{*}=1
where
a:j-1\rightarrow j
for
j=i+2,\cdots,K+1
and
\rho_{a}=1,\rho_{a}^{*}=0
where
a:\infty\rightarrow 0
, while the choice of the maps
\rho_{i-1\rightarrow i}
and

correspond to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(See Figure
2
for

).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

for

The cohomology
\HH(S_{K},\mathbb{Q})
is spanned by
[S_{K}]\in\HH^{0}(S_{K},\mathbb{Q})
and irreducible components of exceptional fibers
[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})]\in\HH^{2}(S_{K},\mathbb{Q})
. On the other hand, since Nakajima quiver varieties are tautologically generated
[
58
]
, it follows that the Chern class of the tautological bundles
c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i})
also spans
\HH^{2}(S_{K},\mathbb{Q})
. For any finite subgroup
G\subset\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, the geometric McKay correspondence
[
31
]
gives a duality between the irreducible components of the exceptional fiber
\pi:S_{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
and the tautological bundles associated to the irreducible representation of the group
G
. Reinterpretating this in terms of the Nakajima quiver variety gives

Proposition 14.3
.

For all
i\in\{1,\cdots,K\}
, we have

[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})]\simeq\sum_{k=1}^{K}c_{ik}c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{k}),

where
c_{ik}\in\End_{K}(\mathbb{Z})
is the Cartan matrix for the
A_{K}
quiver, i,e
c_{ik}=2\delta_{ik}-\delta_{i,k-1}-\delta_{i,k+1}
where
1\leq i,j\leq K
.

Proof.

For each edge
i
where
i\neq 0
, we have tautological morphisms
\rho_{i,i+1}:\mathcal{V}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{i+1}
and
\rho_{i-1,i}^{*}:\mathcal{V}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{i-1}
. So the preprojective relations give rise to a complex of tautological bundles

\mathcal{V}_{i}\xrightarrow{\rho_{i-1,i}^{*}\oplus\rho_{i,i+1}}\mathcal{V}_{i-1}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{\rho_{i-1,i}-\rho_{i,i+1}^{*}}\mathcal{V}_{i}

Thus we have a map between vector bundles

\mathcal{V}_{i}\xrightarrow{\rho_{i-1,i}^{*}\oplus\rho_{i,i+1}}\textrm{Ker}(\rho_{i-1,i}-\rho_{i,i+1}^{*}).

Consider the locus
Z_{i}
where the above map is not an isomorphism. It follows from Example
9
or
[
67
]
[Example 6.3] that
Z_{i}=\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})
. But note that the morphism

\mathcal{V}_{i-1}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{\rho_{i-1,i}-\rho_{i,i+1}^{*}}\mathcal{V}_{i}

is a a surjection. Since it is not a surjection, then it must be
0
. But then this gives a subrepresentation of
\overline{Q}^{K}
which is not
\infty
generated, contradicting the stability. Thus, this morphism is a surjection. But this implies that the fundamental class
[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{reg})]=2c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i})-c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i+1})-c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{i-1})
.
∎

By Proposition
13.1
, we have action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
on

\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\text{H}(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{-}-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d},\fr}(Q),\pPhi_{\Tr(\widetilde{W_{\mathbf{f}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{\hat{\zeta}-\mathrm{ss}}_{\mathbf{d},\fr}(Q^{K})}[1])\simeq\bigoplus_{V\in R(\mathbb{Z}_{K+1})}\text{H}(\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

The induced action of the
\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
can be identified with Nakajima’s operators
e_{i}
. We have

Proposition 14.4
.

The action of generator
\alpha_{i}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta_{i}}

\alpha_{i}:\HH(\Hilb^{V-\rho_{i}}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\rightarrow\HH(\Hilb^{V}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}),

coincides with the action of Nakajima operator
e_{i}
.

Proof.

It is shown in
[
95
]
[Theorem 5.1], that dimension reduction from
\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}}
is compatible with action on Nakajima quiver variety. Furthermore, in
[
94
]
[Theorem 5.6], it is shown that the action of
x_{i,1}^{0}\in\mathbb{C}[x_{i,1}]=\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q},\delta_{i}}
coincides with the action of Nakajima operators.
∎

We also consider the action of any non-zero
\gamma_{\delta}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,-2}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta}
, this make sense since by Proposition
8.2
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,-2}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta}
is a 1 dimensional vector space and
u\cdot\gamma_{\delta}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
. Choose a non zero element
\lvert 0\rangle\in\HH(\Hilb^{0}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\subset\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
. We then have

Proposition 14.5
.

For some non-zero
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have an equality

\gamma_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle=\lambda^{\prime}[S_{K}]

and

\displaystyle(u\cdot\gamma_{\delta})\lvert 0\rangle=\lambda^{\prime}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i})\right)=\lambda^{\prime}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{K}A_{ij}[\mathfrak{B}_{j}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})]\right)

where
A=C^{-1}
, is the inverse of the Cartan matrix of
A_{K}
.

Proof.

The action of
\gamma_{\delta}
gives a map

\gamma_{\delta}\colon\HH(\Hilb^{0}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}))\rightarrow\HH(\Hilb^{\rho_{\textrm{reg}}},\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[2]\oplus\mathbb{Q}^{K}[0]

Since the element
\gamma_{\delta}
is of cohomological degree
-2
, we have
\gamma_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle=\lambda^{\prime}[S]
for some
\lambda^{\prime}
. We claim that
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 0
.

By Theorem
8.8
, the action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
lifts to an action of double
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q^{K}}
. By Example
7
,
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
contains the Lowest weight module
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q^{K}},\hat{\mathbf{0}}}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q^{K}}
where
\hat{\mathbf{0}}:\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\mapsto-\delta_{0}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
. In Example
5
, we calculated
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q^{K}}
and in particular we can identity
\gamma_{\delta}
with Chevalley generator
e_{\delta}
. We also have annihilation operator
f_{\delta}
and element
h_{\delta}
in the Cartan. We can identify the vaccum element
\lvert 0\rangle
as
1\otimes 1\in L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q^{K}},\hat{\mathbf{0}}}
. Since
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{Q^{K}},\hat{\mathbf{0}}}
is a Lowest weight module, by definition
f_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle=0
and
h_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle=-\delta_{i}\cdot\delta\lvert 0\rangle=-\lvert 0\rangle
. But then
e_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle
can’t be
0
, since otherwise
h_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle=[e_{\delta},f_{\delta}]\lvert 0\rangle=0
. Thus
e_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle\neq 0
and hence
\gamma_{\delta}\lvert 0\rangle\neq 0
and thus
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 0
.

Next, by Proposition
13.5
, it follows that
(u\cdot\gamma_{\delta})\star[0]=u\cdot([S])=c_{1}(\mathcal{R})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i})
. Then, the claim follows from Proposition
14.3
.

∎

14.2.
Hilbert schemes

For the minimal resolution
S_{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
, we consider the
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{Z}
graded vector space

V_{K}:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})

and introduce a similarly defined action of a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
and study their relationship with Nakajima’s operators. Note that since
\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})
is smooth, there is an isomorphism between the Borel-Moore homology and the usual cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\simeq\HH(\Hilb^{n}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
.

14.2.1.
Nakajima operators

In
[
64
]
, for any quasi-projective surface
S
, the author constructs the action of certain operators
p_{m}(\alpha)
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\alpha\in\HH(S,\mathbb{Q})
on

V(S):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n}(S),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})

such that they satisfy infinite dimensional Heisenberg Lie algebra type relations. We explain the construction for
m>0
, since that’s what we will be using later. Let

\Hilb^{n,n+m}(S)=\{I\subset J,\Supp(I/J)=\pt\in S\}\subset S\times\Hilb^{n+m}(S)\times\Hilb^{n}(S).

Note that, we have a map
s:\Hilb^{n,n+m}(S)\rightarrow S
given by taking
s(I\subset J)=\Supp(I/J)
. For any
I\subset\{1,2,3\}
, let
\pi_{I}
be the projection maps from
S\times\Hilb^{n+m}(S)\times\Hilb^{n}(S)
to components in
I
. Then

p_{m}(\alpha):\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n}(S),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n+m}(S),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})

is defined to be
p_{m}(\alpha)(c)=(\pi_{2})_{*}((\pi_{1,3})^{*}((PD([S])\cap\alpha)\otimes c)\cap[\Hilb^{n,n+m}(S)])
where
PD:\HH(S,\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(S,\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
is the Poincare duality map. The action of
p_{m}(\alpha)
for
m<0
is defined similarly. Furthermore, the action of operators
p_{n}(\alpha)
for any
n
and
\alpha
is faithful on
V(S)

[
64
]
.

14.2.2.
Lehn and Lin-Qing-Wang relations

In
[
52
]
, the author constructs operators on the Hilbert schemes of points on surfaces by combining Nakajima’s operators and the action of the Chern classes of tautological bundles.

Definition 14.6

(Tautological bundles on Hilbert scheme of points)
.

Let
F
be any locally free sheaf on the smooth quasi-projective surface
S
. For any
n\geq 0
, let
Z^{n}_{S}\subset\Hilb^{n}(S)\times S
be the universal family. The associated tautological bundle
F^{[n]}
is defined to be
(\pi_{1})_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{Z^{n}_{S}}\otimes(\pi_{2})^{*}F)
where
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
are the projection maps from
\Hilb^{n}(S)\times S
. It is a locally free sheaf of rank
rn
on
\Hilb^{n}(S)
where the rank of
F
is
r
. By definition, the fiber
F^{[n]}(Z)
for
Z
a point in
\Hilb^{n}(S)
can be naturally identified with
\HH^{0}(Z,F\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Z})
. When the context is clear, we sometimes abuse notation and refer to the associated tautological bundle
F^{[n]}
as
F
itself.

Definition 14.7

(Lehn operators)
.

Given cohomology class
\alpha\in\HH(S,Q)
, let
p_{n}(\alpha)
be the Nakajima operator. Then we define an operator
p_{n}^{1}(\alpha)=[\partial,p_{n}(\alpha)]
on
V(S)
where
\partial\cdot v=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{S}^{[m]})\cup v
for any
v\in\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{m}(S),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\subset V(S)
and more generally we define

p_{n}^{k}(\alpha):=\textrm{ad}^{k}_{\partial}(p_{n}(\alpha))=[\underbrace{\partial,[\partial,[\cdots,[\partial}_{k\textrm{ times }},p_{n}(\alpha)]]]].

For a smooth projective surface
S
, the relations between the operators
[p_{n}^{k_{n}}(\alpha),p_{m}^{k_{m}}(\beta)]
have been computed in
[
52
]
[Theorem 3.10] and
[
53
]
[Theorem 5.5]. The formula involves contributions of the first Chern of the canonical bundle
c_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{S})
and the Euler class
c_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{S})
.
However, the surface
S_{K}
is only
quasi
-projective in our setting. However, due to the result of
[
58
]
, the cohomology of
S_{K}
is still pure. Furthermore,
S_{K}
is Calabi-Yau, i.e.
\mathcal{K}_{S_{K}}=\mathcal{O}_{S_{K}}
. Let
i:S_{K}\hookrightarrow\overline{S_{K}}
be a smooth compactification. Then the pullback
\HH^{*}(\Hilb^{n}(\overline{S_{K}}),\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\HH^{*}(\Hilb^{n}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q})
is a surjection of cohomology rings. Furthermore, the Chern classes of tautological bundles are supported on the pure cohomology, so that
c_{i}(V)\cup\alpha=c_{i}(V)|_{S_{K}}\cup\alpha
. Since
i^{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{\overline{S_{K}}}))=c_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{S_{K}})=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{S_{K}})=0
and
i^{*}(c_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{\overline{S_{K}}}))=0
by cohomological degree reasons, we conclude from
[
52
]
[Theorem 4.2] and
[
53
]
[Theorem 5.5, 4.2, Definition 5.1] that:

Proposition 14.8
.

For any
v\in V_{K}
, we have:

(1)

For any line bundle
F
on
S_{K}
, we have:

[c_{1}(F),p_{1}(\alpha)]v=p_{1}^{1}(\alpha)v+p_{1}(c_{1}(F)\alpha)v.

(2)

For any
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1
,
a_{1},a_{2}\geq 0
and
\alpha,\beta\in\HH(S_{K},\mathbb{Q})
, we have:

\displaystyle[p^{a_{1}}_{n_{1}}(\alpha),p^{a_{2}}_{n_{2}}(\beta)]v=n_{1}^{a_{1}}n_{2}^{a_{2}}\frac{a_{2}n_{1}-n_{2}a_{1}}{(n_{1}+n_{2})^{a_{1}+a_{2}-1}}p^{a_{1}+a_{2}-1}_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(\alpha\beta)v.

14.2.3.
CoHA subalgebra action

We now consider a subalgebra

\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}^{\Im}:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cdot\delta}\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},n\cdot\delta}

of
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
.
We show that there is an action of
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}^{\Im}
on
V_{K}
.
To do so, we use the work in
[
41
]
, which describes
\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})
as the Nakajima quiver variety on a cyclic quiver with a different stability condition than what we considered before.

Definition 14.9

(Kuznetsov’s stability condition)
.

For any
n\geq 1
, given a dimension vector
n\cdot\delta
, we consider the stability condition
\zeta^{n}
on the cyclic quiver
Q^{K}
defined by
\zeta^{n}_{i}=-2n
for all
i=1,\cdots,K
and
\zeta^{n}_{0}=2Kn-1
.

Note that this stability condition lies in a chamber different from
\zeta^{-}

[
10
]
[Theorem 4.2].
We now show that
\zeta^{n}
stability fits the criterion to apply the Proposition
12.7
.

Proposition 14.10
.

The stability condition
\hat{\zeta^{n}}
is generic for the dimension
(n\cdot\delta,1)
.

Proof.

We have to show that
\mu_{\hat{\zeta^{n}}}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime})\neq\mu_{\hat{\zeta^{n}}}((n\delta,1))
for any
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}<(n\delta,1)
.

•

Assume that
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{d},0)
for some
\mathbf{d}<n\delta
. Then we have
\sum_{i=0}^{K}\zeta_{i}^{n}\mathbf{d}_{i}=0
. Thus

(40)

(2Kn-1)\mathbf{d}_{0}=2n\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathbf{d}_{i}\right).

This implies that
2n
divides
\mathbf{d}_{0}
, which is not possible since
\mathbf{d}_{0}\leq n
unless
\mathbf{d}_{0}=0
. But if
\mathbf{d}_{0}=0
then by the equation
40
,
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathbf{d}_{i}=0
and so each of
\mathbf{d}_{i}=0
.

•

Now assume
\dim(\rho^{\prime})=(\mathbf{d},1)
for some
\mathbf{d}<n\delta
. Then again, by the same argument, we see that it implies
\mathbf{d}=n\cdot\delta
, which contradicts that
\rho^{\prime}
is a subrepresentation.

∎

The reason why we consider this peculiar stability condition is due to the following theorem.

Proposition 14.11
.

[
50
]
[Theorem 4.3]
For any
n\geq 0
,
there is a canonical isomorphism of varieties

g_{n}:\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})\simeq\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{n}}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K}).

Let
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\zeta^{n}}
be the tautological bundles on the Nakajima quiver variety
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{n}}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
. Note that since
S_{K}\simeq\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
there are tautological bundles
\mathcal{V}_{i}
on
S_{K}
, which by Definition
14.6
gives tautological bundles
[\mathcal{V}_{i}]^{[n]}
on
\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})
. We then have

Proposition 14.12
.

Under the above isomorphism, the tautological vector bundles
\mathcal{V}^{\zeta^{n}}_{i}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{n}}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
are pulled back to vector bundles
[\mathcal{V}_{i}]^{[n]}
on
\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})
.

Proof.

This is really in the construction of the isomorphism. Let
Z
be a closed point in
\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})
. It is a length
n
subscheme in
S_{K}
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
be its structure sheaf. Then the map
g_{n}
associates a closed point on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{n}}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
by assigning vector space
V_{i}(Z):=\HH^{0}(S_{K},\mathcal{V}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Z})
to the vertex
i
where
i=0,\cdots,K
,
\HH^{0}(S_{K},\mathcal{O}_{S_{K}})
to the vertex
\infty
. Since the fiber of
(\mathcal{V}_{i})^{[n]}
on any point
Z
is exactly
\HH^{0}(S_{K},\mathcal{V}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Z})
the statement follows. ∎

By Proposition
12.7
, there is an isomorphism of graded vector spaces:

\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathrm{H}(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K}),\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta^{n}}(\widetilde{W}_{\delta_{0}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})}[-1])\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\text{H}^{\text{BM}}(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{n}}_{(n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0})}(Q^{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\simeq V_{K}.

We now mimic the construction as in Section
13
. For any
n,m\geq 0
, we consider the stack of short exact sequences

\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n+m},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,m\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})=\{0\rightarrow(\rho_{1})_{(n\cdot\delta,0)}\rightarrow(\rho_{2})^{\zeta^{n+m}-\mathrm{ss}}_{((m+n)\cdot\delta,1)}\rightarrow(\rho_{3})_{(m\cdot\delta,0)}\rightarrow 0\},

where
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
are representations of
\widetilde{Q}_{\delta_{0}}
of dimension
(n\cdot\delta,0),((m+n)\cdot\delta,1),(m\cdot\delta,1)
respectively such that
\rho_{2}
is
\zeta^{n+m}
-semistable. We then have

Lemma 14.13
.

Let
\rho
be a representation of dimension
((n+m)\cdot\delta,1)
such that
\rho
is
\zeta^{n+m}
-semistable then any quotient
\rho^{\prime}
of dimension
(m\cdot\delta,1)
is
\zeta^{m}
-semistable.

Proof.

Let
\pi:\rho\rightarrow\rho^{\prime}
be the surjection. Let
V\subset\rho^{\prime}
be a non-zero proper subrepresentation such that
V\neq\rho^{\prime}
. Define
\chi_{m}((\mathbf{d},a))=(\sum_{i=0}^{K}\zeta^{m}_{i}\mathbf{d}_{i})+\zeta^{m}_{\infty}a
. Recall(Section
3.9
) that the slope of the representation
V
is defined as
\mu_{\zeta^{m}}(V)=\chi_{m}((\mathbf{d},a))/(\sum_{i=1}^{i=K}\mathbf{d}_{i}+a)
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\chi_{m}(V)\leq 0
. We consider the representation
\pi^{-1}(V)
.

•

Assume that
\dim(V)=(\mathbf{d},1)
. Then
\dim(\pi^{-1}(V))=(\textbf{d}+n\cdot\delta,1)
and is a non-zero proper subrepresentation of
\rho
. Since
\rho
is
\zeta^{n+m}
-semistable, it follows that
\chi_{n+m}(\pi^{-1}(V))<0
. So

\displaystyle 0>\chi_{n+m}(\pi^{-1}(V))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{K}\left(\zeta^{n+m}_{i}(\mathbf{d}_{i}+n)-\zeta^{m+n}_{i}(m+n)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(-2(n+m)(\mathbf{d}_{i}-m)\right)+(2K(m+n)-1)(\mathbf{d}_{0}-m)

\displaystyle=-2(n+m)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})\right)+m-\mathbf{d}_{0}

But then since
V\subset\rho^{\prime}
is a proper subrepresentation,
\mathbf{d}<m\cdot\delta
. The above inequality implies that

\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})>(m-\mathbf{d}_{0})/2(n+m)>0

But since
\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})
is an integer, above inequality implies that in fact
\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})\geq 1
.
So

\displaystyle\chi_{m}(V)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{K}\zeta_{i}^{m}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-m)

\displaystyle=(-2m)\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})+m-\mathbf{d}_{0}\leq-m-\mathbf{d}_{0}<0.

•

Now assume that
\dim(V)=(\textbf{d},0)
. Then again

\displaystyle 0>\chi_{n+m}(\pi^{-1}(V))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{K}\zeta^{n+m}_{i}(\mathbf{d}_{i}+n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(-2(n+m)(\mathbf{d}_{i}+n)\right)+(2K(n+m)-1)(\mathbf{d}_{0}+n)

\displaystyle=-2(n+m)(\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0}))-n-\mathbf{d}_{0}

and so
\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})>-(n+\mathbf{d}_{0})/(2(n+m))\geq-(n+m)(2(n+m))>-1/2
, since
\mathbf{d}_{0}\leq n
. But then since
\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})
is an integer, in fact we have
\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0})\geq 0
. Now

\displaystyle\chi_{m}(V)=\sum_{i=0}^{K}\zeta_{i}^{m}(\mathbf{d}_{i})=-2m(\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{d}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{0}))-\mathbf{d}_{0}\leq 0.

So for every non-zero proper subrepresentation
V\subset\rho^{\prime}
,
\chi_{m}(V)\leq 0
and so
\rho^{\prime}
is
\zeta^{m}
-semistable.
∎

Thus we have the usual correspondence

\hbox to398.91pt{\vbox to20.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 199.45378pt\lower-10.55115pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{91.25377pt}{-6.07892pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 126.27 -8.41)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The map
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3}
is smooth since it is a fibre bundle. Unlike the
\zeta^{-}
stability condition, it is not entirely obvious why the morphism
\pi_{2}
is a proper map. However, by the previous lemma, fibres of the map
\pi_{2}
are Quot schemes, and so, in particular, the morphism
\pi_{2}
is proper. Thus again, just as in the definition of cohomological Hall algebra, we do the pushforward (
5.4
) along
\pi_{2}
and the pullback (
5.2
) along
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3}
in the vanishing cycle cohomology and finally apply the Thom-Sebastiani isomorphism (
5.1
) to get that

Proposition 14.14
.

There is a left action
\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
on the cohomologically graded vector space
V_{K}
. It satisfies
(u\cdot\alpha)\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v=u\cdot(\alpha\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v)-\alpha\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}(u\cdot v)
where action of
u
on
v\in\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\subset V_{K}
is given by cup product with
\sum_{i=0}^{K}c_{1}(\mathcal{V}^{[n]}_{i})
.

Proof.

The associativity of the action can be proven in the same way as the associativity of the cohomological Hall algebra in
[
48
]
. The action of
u
is induced by the determinant line on the Nakajima quiver variety realization in Proposition
14.11
. Thus, the claim follows from Proposition
14.12
.
∎

Thus we have an induced left action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
on
V_{K}
where

\mathfrak{g}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},n\cdot\delta}.

Proposition 14.15
.

The action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
on the graded vector space
V_{K}
is faithful.

Proof.

We first claim that there is isomorphism of graded vector spaces

\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},(n\cdot\delta,1)}.

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta^{n}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}(\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}(\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta_{n},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}(\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textrm{JH}^{\zeta^{n}}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}^{\zeta^{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textrm{JH}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}}

where
q
is the affinization map and
\JH
are the Jordan-Holder maps. Note that
\JH^{\zeta^{n}}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}
is a
\pt/\mathbb{C}^{*}
fibre bundle and thus we have

(\JH^{\zeta^{n}}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta^{n}}(\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta^{n}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}(\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}\simeq\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta^{n}}(\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})}[-1]\otimes\HH(\BCu,\mathbb{Q}).

So, in particular,

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}^{\zeta^{n}}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}={}^{\mathfrak{p^{\prime}}}\!\tau^{\leq 1}((\JH^{\zeta^{n}}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)})_{*}\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta^{n}}(\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta^{n}-\mathrm{ss}}_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}(\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}[1])\simeq\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta^{n}}(\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})}.

By the theorem of Toda (Proposition
6.3
), we have a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\HH(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K}),\mathcal{BPS}^{\zeta^{n}}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}[-1])\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},(n\cdot\delta,1)}.

Finally, by Proposition
12.7
, there is an isomorphism

\HH(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n},3d}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K}),\pPhi_{\Tr^{\zeta_{n}}(\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}})}\mathcal{IC}_{\mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{n}}_{n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})}[-1])\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{n}}_{(n\cdot\delta,\delta_{0})}(Q^{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

and thus the claim follows from Proposition
14.11
.

So we may identify the action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
on
V_{K}\simeq\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},(n\cdot\delta,1)}
by the natural identification
\mathfrak{g}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}\simeq\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},(n\cdot\delta,0)}
. Let
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},(m\cdot\delta,0)}
for some
m\geq 0
. By Theorem
8.8
, we may identify
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}
as a positive Half of the generalized Kac Moody Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}
. Note that
(0\cdot\delta,1)
is a primitive positive root of
\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}
, since the Symmetrized Euler form
((0\cdot\delta,1),(0\cdot\delta,1))_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}=2
. Thus there exist Chevalley generators for dimension
(0\cdot\delta,1)
in the subspace
\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},(n\cdot\delta,1)}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\GKM}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}
. We denote them by
e_{\infty},f_{\infty}
and
h_{\infty}
where
e_{\infty}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},\widetilde{W^{K}_{\delta_{0}}},(0\cdot\delta,1)}
. We claim that
[\alpha,e_{\infty}]\neq 0
, which proves the assertion. Note that
[\alpha,f_{\infty}]=0
as there are no elements in dimension
(m\cdot\delta,-1)
. Also by definition of Generalized Kac Moody Lie algebra,
[\alpha,h_{\infty}]=((m\cdot\delta,0),(0\cdot\delta,1))_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}_{\delta_{0}}}}=-h_{\infty}\neq 0
. But then if
[\alpha,e_{\infty}]=0
, then
[\alpha,[f_{\infty},e_{\infty}]]=0=[\alpha,h_{\infty}]=0
which we checked is non-zero.

∎

Proposition 14.16
.

Up to a non-zero scalar, the operator
\gamma_{\delta}
acts on
V_{K}
as the Nakajima raising operator
p_{1}([S_{K}])
.

Proof.

Applying dimension reduction (
5.5
) by forgetting loops, i.e. the morphism of representation spaces
\Rep_{\delta}(\widetilde{Q})=\Rep_{\delta}(\overline{Q^{K}})\times\Rep_{\delta}(L^{K})\rightarrow\Rep_{\delta}(\overline{Q^{K}})
, where
L^{K}
is the quiver obtained by removing all the arrows
a
and
a^{*}
from the tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q^{K}}
, The action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta}
on
V_{K}
gives a map of cohomologically graded vector spaces

a_{1,n}:\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\mathfrak{M}_{\delta}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\times\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{n+1}(S_{K}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

We consider the stability condition
\zeta^{1}
(Definition
14.9
) so that
\mathfrak{M}_{\delta}^{\zeta^{1}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}})\simeq\Coh_{\pt}(S)\simeq S/\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Then by Toda’s theorem (Proposition
6.3
),
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,\zeta^{1}}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}\delta}
, we have an isomorphism of vector spaces
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(S_{K},\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
. By observing the cohomological degree, we can identify
\gamma_{\delta}
with
[S_{K}]\in\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(S_{K},\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
. Let

\mathcal{M}_{(n,n+1)}^{\text{st}}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q}):=\{0\rightarrow(\rho_{1})_{\delta}\rightarrow(\rho_{2})_{(n+1)\cdot\delta,1}\rightarrow(\rho_{3})_{(n\cdot\delta,1)}\rightarrow 0\}

be the variety of short exact sequences on representations of
\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q_{\delta_{0}}}
such that
\rho_{1}
is
\zeta^{1}
-semistable,
\rho_{2}
is
\zeta^{n+1}
-semistable and
\rho_{3}
is
\zeta^{n}
stable. Given a point
(Z_{1}\subset Z_{2})\in\Hilb^{n,n+1}(S_{K})
, it induces a short exact sequence of sheaves

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Z_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\rightarrow 0

for some
s\in S_{K}
which equivalently after applying
g
from Proposition
14.11
, gives an exact sequence of representations

0\rightarrow\widehat{g_{1}(s)}\rightarrow g_{n+1}(Z_{2})\rightarrow g_{n}(Z_{1})\rightarrow 0

where
\widehat{g_{1}(s)}
is the kernel of the surjection
g_{1}(s)\rightarrow\rho_{0}
where
\rho_{0}
is unique
(\textbf{0},1)
dimensional representation. The same can be done the other way, i.e., given a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow\rho_{1}\rightarrow\rho_{2}\rightarrow\rho_{3}\rightarrow 0
in
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{st}}_{(n,n+1)}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q^{K}})
, we can apply the inverse of isomorphism
g
to get a closed point
g_{n}^{-1}(\rho_{3})\subset g_{n+1}^{-1}(\rho_{2})
of
\Hilb^{n,n+1}(S_{K})
. Thus, it follows that
\Hilb^{n,n+1}(S_{K})\simeq\mathcal{M}^{\text{st}}_{(n,n+1)}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})
. We then consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{K}\times\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{\text{st}}_{(n,n+1)}(\mathrm{\Pi}_{Q})\simeq\Hilb^{n,n+1}(S_{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hilb^{n+1}(S_{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{K}\times\Hilb^{n+1}(S_{K})\times\Hilb^{n}(S_{K})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}\times p_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

where the morphism
p_{1}\times p_{3}
is projection to
(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})
and
p_{2}
is projection to
\rho_{2}
. In particular,
p_{1}\times p_{3}
is already quasi-smooth, and
p_{2}
is proper. However, since dimension reduction is compatible with the action of the cohomological Hall algebra of preprojective algebra on Nakajima quiver varieties
[
95
]
19
19

19

In particular, since
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{3}
is smooth, we use the compatibility between dimension reduction with pullback and pushforward.
), we have

\displaystyle a_{1,n}([S_{K}]\otimes\alpha)=(p_{2})_{*}(p_{1}\times p_{3})^{!}([S_{K}]\otimes\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{2})_{*}(\pi_{1,3}^{*}([S_{K}]\otimes\alpha)\cap[\Hilb^{n,n+1}(S_{K})])

\displaystyle=p_{1}([S_{K}])\alpha,

as we wanted.

∎

15.
Integral Matrix
\mathcal{W}_{1+\infty}
Lie algebras

In the introduction, we defined the associative algebras
D_{\hbar}(\mathbb{C}^{*})
and
D_{\hbar}(\mathbb{C}^{*})\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{K}
for any
K\geq 1
(Definition
2.1
). We further defined a subspace
\mathcal{W}_{K}\subset(D_{\hbar}(\mathbb{C}^{*})\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{K})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar]}\mathbb{C}[\hbar^{\pm 1}]
. We check that

Proposition 15.1
.

The subspace
\mathcal{W}_{K}\subset(D_{\hbar}(\mathbb{C}^{*})\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{K})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar]}\mathbb{C}[\hbar^{-1}]
forms a Lie subalgebra.

Proof.

We recall that
\mathcal{W}_{K}
is the
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
linear subspace spanned by
T_{m,a}(X)=z^{m}D^{a}X
where
m\in\mathbb{Z},a\geq 0
, and
t_{m,a}=T_{m,a}(1)/\hbar
where
1\in\mathfrak{gl}_{K}
is the identity matrix.
Since
[D,z]=\hbar z
and
[D,z^{-1}]=-\hbar z^{-1}
it follows inductively that

(41)

\displaystyle[z^{m}D^{a},z^{n}D^{b}]=z^{m+n}((D+n\hbar)^{a}D^{b}-D^{a}(D+m\hbar)^{b}).

Since the expression on the right is of the form
\hbar\cdot(\textrm{some linear expression in }t_{p,q})
, it shows that the Lie brackets
[t_{m,a},t_{n,b}]
are closed. We can similarly check for the Lie brackets of type
[t_{m,a},T_{n,b}(X)]
since

(42)

\displaystyle[z^{m}D^{a},z^{n}D^{b}X]=z^{m+n}((D+n\hbar)^{a}D^{b}-D^{a}(D+m\hbar)^{b})X.

The other Lie brackets are clearly closed since
D_{\hbar}(\mathbb{C}^{*})\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{K}
forms a Lie algebra.
∎

We now define the positive half of
\mathcal{W}_{K}
, by mimicking the definition of standard positive half of the affine Lie algebra.

Definition 15.2

(Positive half of
\mathcal{W}_{K}
)
.

The positive half
(\mathcal{W}_{K})^{+}
is defined to be
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
linear subalgebra generated by
T_{k,a}(X)
where
k\geq 1,a\geq 0,X\in\mathfrak{gl}_{K}
or
k=0,a\geq 0,X\in\mathfrak{n}_{K}
where
\mathfrak{n}_{K}\subset\mathfrak{gl}_{K}
is the Lie subalgebra generated by upper diagonal matrices and
t_{k,a}
where
k\geq 1,a\geq 0
.

We will also consider the classical limit of
\mathcal{W}_{K}
. Note, however, that the way it is defined, we cannot directly set
\hbar=0
since
t_{n,a}=T_{n,a}(1)/\hbar
. But using relations (
41
) and (
42
) and more generally

(43)

\displaystyle[z^{m}D^{a}X,z^{n}D^{b}Y]=z^{m+n}(D+n\hbar)^{a}D^{b}XY-z^{m+n}D^{a}(D+m\hbar)^{b}YX,

for any
X,Y\in\mathfrak{gl}_{K}
, it follows that

Proposition 15.3
.

Let
\widetilde{W}_{K}
be the Lie algebra generated by
T_{k,a}(X)
where
X\in\mathfrak{sl}_{K}
and
t_{k,a}
where
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\geq 0
with the relations

(44)

\displaystyle[t_{m,a},T_{n,b}(X)]

\displaystyle=(na-mb)T_{m+n,a+b-1}(X)

(45)

\displaystyle[t_{m,a},t_{n,b}]

\displaystyle=(na-mb)t_{m+n,a+b-1}

(46)

\displaystyle[T_{m,a}(X),T_{n,b}(Y)]

\displaystyle=T_{m+n,a+b}([X,Y])

Then there is an isomorphism of Lie algebras

(\mathcal{W}_{K})/(\hbar=0)\simeq\widetilde{W}_{K}

Recall the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{po}(\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*}))\ltimes(\mathcal{O}(\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*}))
defined in Section
2.1
. We note that

\mathfrak{po}(\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*}))\ltimes(\mathcal{O}(\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*}))\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{K}))\simeq\widetilde{W}_{K},

since
\mathfrak{po}(\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*}))
, the Lie algebra of the functions on the cotangent bundle
\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*})
is generated by
D=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
and
z^{\pm 1}
with the Poisson bracket, determined by
\{D,z\}=z
. It is spanned by
z^{k}D^{a}
where
k\in\mathbb{Z},a\geq 0
and the morphism

\mathrm{A}\colon\mathfrak{po}(\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*}))\ltimes(\mathcal{O}(\TT^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{*}))\rightarrow\widetilde{W}_{K},

given by
\mathrm{A}(z^{k_{1}}D^{a_{1}},z^{k_{2}}D^{a_{2}}X)\mapsto t_{k_{1},a_{1}}+T_{k_{2},a_{2}}(X)
is an isomorphism of Lie algebras. We now define the positive half of
\widetilde{W}_{K}
in the same way as before.

Definition 15.4

(Positive half of
\widetilde{W}_{K}
)
.

Let
\widetilde{W}_{K}^{+}\subset\widetilde{W}_{K}
be the Lie subalgebra spanned by
T_{k,a}(X)
where
k\geq 1,a\geq 0,X\in\mathfrak{sl}_{K}
or
k=0,a\geq 0,X\in\mathfrak{n}_{K}
where
\mathfrak{n}_{K}\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{K}
is the Lie subalgebra generated by upper diagonal matrices and
t_{k,a}
where
k\geq 1,a\geq 0
.

We now show that
\widetilde{W}_{K}
is, in fact, a Lie algebra in the category of
Heis
modules. Let

H_{K}=\sum(i-1/2-K/2)E_{i,i}

be a diagonal element inside
\mathfrak{sl}_{K}
. We defined it so that
[H_{K},E_{i,j}]=(i-j)E_{i,j}
for
E_{i,j}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{K}
.

Definition 15.5

(Grading on
\widetilde{W}_{K}^{+}
)
.

The Lie algebra
\widetilde{W}_{K}^{+}
has two gradings. We define the
cohomological grading
by
\mathrm{CG}(T_{m,a}(X))=2a,\mathrm{CG}(t_{m,a})=2a-2
and
dimension grading
induced by
\dim(T_{m,a}(E_{ij}))=Km+j-i
and
\dim(t_{m,a})=Km
.

Proposition 15.6
.

There is an action of
\mathbf{Heis}
on
\widetilde{W}_{K}^{+}
by derivations, given by

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left[\frac{t_{0,2}}{2}-\frac{T_{0,1}(H_{K})}{K},-\right]

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\rightarrow\partial_{D}

where
\partial_{D}(T_{m,a}(X))=aKT_{m,a-1}(X),\partial_{D}(t_{m,a})=aKt_{m,a-1}
. For every graded piece of fixed dimension
\dim
, the central charge is the dimension
\dim
, and the Lie algebra
\widetilde{W}_{K}^{+}
is negatively determined.

Proof.

Since
p
acts as a commutator, it is a derivation. The same can be checked for
q
using Lie algebra relations (
44
). On the other hand,

\displaystyle[q,p](T_{m,a}(E_{i,j}))

\displaystyle=(mK+(j-i))T_{m,a}(E_{i,j})

\displaystyle[q,p](t_{m,a})

\displaystyle=mKt_{m,a}

The negatively determined assumption holds since the
\mathbf{Heis}
grading is the cohomological grading and
\partial_{D}
is injective for
a\geq 1
.

∎

Next, we see that
\widetilde{W}_{K}^{+}
is generated by a much smaller set of generators.

Proposition 15.7
.

The Lie algebra
\widetilde{W}_{K}^{+}
is generated as a Lie algebra by the elements
t_{1,a}
for
a\geq 0
,
T_{0,a}(E_{i,i+1})
for
i=1,2,\cdots,K-1;a\geq 0
and
T_{1,a}(E_{1,K}),a\geq 0
.

Proof.

Since
[t_{1,0},t_{n,b}]=(-b)t_{n+1,b-1}
, the elements
t_{n,a},n\geq 1;a\geq 0
are in the subalgebra generated by
t_{1,a}
where
a\geq 0
. The elements
E_{i,i+1}
for
i\in[1,K-1]
and
zE_{K,1}
are the generators of the positive half of the affine Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{sl}}_{K}
, which is
\mathfrak{n}_{K}\oplus z\mathbb{Q}[z]\mathfrak{sl}_{K}
. Since
[T_{m,a}(X),T_{n,b}(Y)]=T_{m+n,a+b}([X,Y])
, it follows that all
T_{k,a}(X)
where
k\geq 1,a\geq 0,X\in\mathfrak{sl}_{K}
or
k=0,a\geq 0,X\in\mathfrak{n}_{K}
are in the algebra generated by
T_{0,a}(E_{i,i+1})
for
i=1,2,\cdots,K-1;a\geq 0
and
T_{1,a}(E_{1,K}),a\geq 0
and so we are done.
∎

16.
Affinized BPS Lie algebra of Cyclic Quivers

We are ready to calculate the affinized BPS Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{{K}}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
for
K\geq 1
. The affinized BPS Lie algebra for the cyclic quiver
Q^{K}
is spanned by
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}^{(n)}
where
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
. It suffices to understand the Lie bracket between these generators. By Proposition
8.2
, we have an isomorphism of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}\simeq\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}\oplus s\mathbb{Q}[s]
. Let
\mathfrak{re}_{K}\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{{K}}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
be the subvector space spanned by the elements
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}^{(n)}
for
n\geq 0
, where
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}\simeq\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{K}}
. It follows from Theorem
11.2
that

Proposition 16.1
.

We have an isomorphism of Lie algebras

\mathfrak{re}_{K}\simeq\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{K}}[D].

Next, let
\mathfrak{im}_{K}\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{{K}}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
be the subvector space generated by the elements
\alpha^{(n)}_{m\cdot\delta}
where
\alpha_{m\cdot\delta}
spans
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,-2}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},m\cdot\delta}\simeq\mathbb{C}
. Since
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,-2}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},m\cdot\delta}
is one dimensional,
\alpha_{m\cdot\delta}
is uniquely determined up to a non-zero scalar.

Proposition 16.2
.

For any
n\geq 1
, there exist some non-zero scalar
\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that we have an equality

[\alpha_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{n\cdot\delta}]=\lambda_{n}\alpha_{(n+1)\cdot\delta}.

Proof.

Note that the Lie bracket
[\alpha_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{n\cdot\delta}]
is an element of cohomological degree
-2
in the dimension
(n+1)\cdot\delta
. Since
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,-2}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},(n+1)\cdot\delta}\simeq\mathbb{Q}
is a one dimensional vector space, it is either
0
or up to a non-zero scalar, it is
\alpha_{(n+1)\cdot\delta}
. We claim that it is non-zero. We consider the action
\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}
on the cohomology of Hilbert scheme of
S_{K}
constructed in Proposition
14.14
and show that the Lie bracket
[\alpha_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{n\cdot\delta}]
acts non-trivially. We claim that for any
n\geq 1
, up to a factor of a non-zero scalar, there is an equality

\alpha_{n\cdot\delta}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v=p_{n}([S_{K}])v

where
p_{n}([S_{K})])
is Nakajima’s operator (See Section
14.2.1
) and
v\in V_{K}
. The case when
n=1
is proved in Proposition
14.16
. Then by Proposition
14.8

(47)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\delta}^{(1)}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v=\sum_{i=0}^{K}[c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i}),p_{1}([S_{K}])]v

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{K}(p_{1}^{1}([S_{K}])v+p_{1}(c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i}))v)

\displaystyle=(K+1)p_{1}^{1}([S_{K}])v+\sum_{i=0}^{K}p_{1}(c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i}))v

where for each
i
,
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is the tautological bundle on
S_{K}
. But then, assuming the claim for
n=N
, we have

\displaystyle[\alpha_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{N\cdot\delta}]\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v

\displaystyle=(K+1)[p_{1}^{1}([S_{K}]),p_{N}([S_{K}])]v+\sum_{i=0}^{K}[p_{1}(c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i})),p_{N}([S_{K}])]v

\displaystyle=-(N)(K+1)p_{N+1}([S_{K}])v,

The second equality is again a consequence of Proposition
14.8
. But then for any
n
, there exists some
v\in V_{K}
such that
p_{N+1}([S_{K}])v\neq 0
, since Nakajima’s operators act faithfully (see Section
14.2.1
), and thus
[\alpha_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{N\cdot\delta}]\neq 0
and thus up to a factor of non-zero scalar
[\alpha_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{N\cdot\delta}]=\alpha_{(n+1)\cdot\delta}
and both the claim and proposition follows by induction.
∎

From now on, we fix
\gamma_{\delta}
as some renormalization of
\alpha_{\delta}
, so that for the vacuum vector
\lvert 0\rangle=[0]\in\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(\Hilb^{0},\mathbb{Q}_{0})\in\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
we have
\gamma_{\delta}\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=[S_{K}]
. This is allowed by Proposition
14.5
. Then we have
\gamma_{\delta}^{(r)}=u^{r}\cdot\gamma_{\delta}
. Finally for all
k\geq 1
, we fix

(48)

\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}:=\ad^{k-1}_{\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}}(\gamma_{\delta})

which by the previous propositon, is just the renormalization of
\alpha_{k\cdot\delta}
and it spans
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,-2}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
. Similarly, we have

\gamma^{(r)}_{k\cdot\delta}:=u^{r}\ad^{k-1}_{\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}}(\gamma_{\delta}^{(0)}).

Now we study how an element of
\mathfrak{im}_{K}
interacts with an element of
\mathfrak{re}_{K}
. By
[a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\cdots,a_{N}]
we mean the iterated Lie bracket
[a_{1},[a_{2},[a_{3},[\cdots,a_{N}]]]]
.

Proposition 16.3
.

For any
i\in[0,K]
, the Lie bracket
[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]\neq 0
.

Proof.

We consider the action of
\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},W_{K}}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
as constructed in Section
13
. We show that
[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle\neq 0
. By Proposition
14.4
, we can identify the action of
\alpha_{i}
with the action of the generator
e_{i}
of the affine Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{sl}}_{K+1}
on
\mathrm{N}^{\zeta^{\infty}}_{\delta_{0}}(Q^{K})
, due to Nakajima (Section
14.1.3
).

Note that
\Hilb^{\rho_{0}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})=\pt
as
I=\langle x,y\rangle
is the only
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
equivariant ideal with
\mathbb{C}[x,y]/I\simeq\rho_{0}
. Thus
\mathfrak{B}_{0}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})=\Hilb^{\rho_{0}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Let
[0_{[0,1)}]\in\HH(\Hilb^{\rho_{0}}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
be the fundamental class of the point. Thus by (
38
), we have

\alpha_{0}\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=[0_{[0,1)}].

Similarly, since the closed points of
\Hilb^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
are just the maximial ideals
(x-t_{1},y-t_{2})
, only
\mathbb{Z}_{K+1}
equivariant point is the ideal
(x,y)
. It follows that
\Hilb^{\rho_{i}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})=0
for all
i\neq 0
and so by (
38
), for all
i\neq 0
we have

\alpha_{i}\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=0.

Now for any
i\neq K
, we consider the equivariant Hilbert scheme
\Hilb^{\oplus_{j=0}^{i}\rho_{j}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. By Proposition
14.1
,
\Hilb^{\oplus_{j=0}^{i}\rho_{j}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
is
0
dimensional connected scheme and thus just a point. Let
[0_{[0,i)}]\in\HH(\Hilb^{\oplus_{j=0}^{i}\rho_{j}}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
be the fundamental class of the point. Consider
\alpha_{1}\bullet(\alpha_{0}\bullet\lvert 0\rangle)
. Again since each of the schemes
\Hilb^{\rho_{0}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
\Hilb^{\rho_{0}\oplus\rho_{1}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
are points and
\rho_{0}\subset\rho_{0}\oplus\rho_{1}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{B}_{1}(\rho_{0})=\Hilb^{\rho_{0}\oplus\rho_{1}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. We thus have by (
38
) that

\alpha_{1}\bullet(\alpha_{0}\bullet\lvert 0\rangle)=[0_{[0,2)}].

We may continue this argument succesively to calculate
\alpha_{i-1}\bullet(\alpha_{i-2}\bullet(\cdots\alpha_{0}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}\lvert 0\rangle))
for any
i\in[1,K]
. By associativity of the action of the
\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}
, we conclude that for any
i\in[1,K]
,

(\alpha_{i-1}\star\alpha_{i-2}\star\cdots\star\alpha_{0})\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=[0_{[0,i)}]\in\HH(\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0}^{i}\rho_{j})}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}).

Now consider the equivariant Hilbert scheme
\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0}^{i}\rho_{j})\oplus\rho_{K}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Again, by Proposition
14.1
, it is a
0
-dimensional connected scheme and hence a point. Let
[0_{[K,i+1)}]\in\HH(\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0}^{i}\rho_{j})\oplus\rho_{K}}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
be the fundamental class of the point. Then again by (
38
), we have

\alpha_{K}\bullet((\alpha_{i-1}\star\alpha_{i-2}\star\cdots\star\alpha_{0})\bullet\lvert 0\rangle)=(-1)[0_{[K,i+1)}]\in\HH(\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0}^{i}\rho_{j})\oplus\rho_{K}}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

Note that the sign comes from the sign in the formula (
38
). For any
i\in[1,K]
, we now consider
\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0,j\neq i}^{K}\rho_{j})}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Again,
\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0,j\neq i}^{K}\rho_{j})}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
is a point by Proposition
14.1
. Let
[0_{[i+1,K)}]\in\HH(\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0,j\neq i}^{K}\rho_{j})}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
be the fundamental class of the point. Then by the same argument as above, we conclude for any
i\in[1,K]
, that

(\alpha_{i+1}\star\alpha_{i+2}\star\cdots\alpha_{K}\star\alpha_{i-1}\star\cdots\alpha_{0})\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=(-1)^{K-i}[0_{[i+1,K)}]\in\HH(\Hilb^{(\oplus_{j=0,j\neq i}^{K}\rho_{j})}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

Note that
\rho_{\textrm{reg}}-\rho_{i}=\oplus_{j=0,j\neq i}^{K}\rho_{j}
. By (
38
), for
i\in[1,K]
, we have
\alpha_{i}\bullet[0_{[i+1,K)}]=(-1)[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})]
, where by
39
, we may view
\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})
as a Lagrangian inside the surface
S_{K}
. In particular
[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})]\in\HH(S_{K},\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})
. We thus have for
i\in[1,K]
,

(\alpha_{i}\star\alpha_{i+1}\cdots\star\alpha_{K}\star\alpha_{i-1}\cdots\star\alpha_{0})\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=(-1)^{K+1-i}[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})].

But since
\alpha_{i}\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=0
for
i\neq 0
, we can write above as

(49)

\displaystyle[\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1}\cdots,\alpha_{K},\alpha_{i-1}\cdots,\alpha_{0}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=(-1)^{K+1-i}[\mathfrak{B}_{i}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})].

Now for
i\neq 0
, we consider the commutator
[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]
. Again since
\alpha_{i}\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=0
, we have by Proposition
14.5
that

\displaystyle[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]\lvert 0\rangle=\alpha_{i}\bullet\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}\lvert 0\rangle=\alpha_{i}\star(\sum_{j}(\sum_{k}A_{kj})[\mathfrak{B}_{j}(\rho_{\textrm{reg}})]).

But by the Equation
49
, this is the same as

[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]\lvert 0\rangle=\sum_{j}(-1)^{K+1-j}\sum_{k}A_{kj}[\alpha_{i};\mathcal{B}_{j}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle,

where
[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{j}]
represents the commutator

[\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j+1}\cdots,\alpha_{K},\alpha_{j-1}\cdots,\alpha_{0}].

and
A_{k,j}
are the entries of the inverse of the Cartan matrix of
A_{K}
. It is easy to calculate the inverse of the Cartan matrix of
A_{K}
. We have
A_{kj}=\min(k,j)-\frac{kj}{K+1}
, which shows that
S_{j}=\sum_{k}A_{kj}=j(K+1-j)/2
. Similarly, since
\mathfrak{n}_{Q^{K}}^{+}\simeq(\mathfrak{sl}_{K+1}[z,z^{-1}])^{+}
after identifying
e_{i}
with
E_{i,i+1}
and
e_{0}
with
zE_{K+1,1}
; we may identify
[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}]=2((-1)^{K-i}zE_{i,i+1})
while
[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i-1}]=[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i+1}]=(-1)^{K-i}zE_{i,i+1}
and
[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{j}]=0
, otherwise. In particular
[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}]=2[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i-1}]=2[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i+1}]
. Thus we have

\displaystyle[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=(-1)^{K+1-i}(S_{i}-\frac{S_{i-1}+S_{i+1}}{2})[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=(-1)^{(K+1-i)}/2[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle.

But
[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle\neq 0
by the same argument for faithfulness as in Proposition
14.5
. This in particular means that
[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]\neq 0
. By symmetry, we also get the same relation for
i=0
, since we can construct the representation by adding the framing to vertex
1
instead of
0
. Thus we have

\displaystyle[\alpha_{0},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle=\frac{(-1)^{K}}{2}[\alpha_{0},\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}]\bullet\lvert 0\rangle\neq 0

and so we are done.
∎

Note that
[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]
is an element of cohomological degree
0
, in dimension
\delta+\delta_{i}
. From proposition
16.3
, it follows that
[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]=\lambda[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}]
for some non-zero
\lambda
, since
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\delta+\delta_{i}}
is
1
dimensional. But then, the above calculation even precisely determines
\lambda
, and so finally, we have

(50)

[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}]=\frac{(-1)^{K+1-i}}{2}[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}].

Furthermore, since
[\alpha_{i},\gamma_{\delta}^{(0)}]=0
, we have
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(0)},\alpha_{i}^{(1)}]=\frac{(-1)^{K+1-i}}{2}[\alpha_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}]
.

Corollary 16.3.1

(Strong rationality for Kleinian singularity)
.

For any
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
, the commutator

[\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)},\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt]:\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\chi}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}

maps
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\mathbf{d}}
isomorphically to
\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}},\mathbf{d}+\delta}
.

Proof.

The Lie algebra generated by
\alpha_{i}
where
i\in[0,K]
is isomorphic to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{Q^{K}}\simeq(\mathfrak{sl}_{K+1}[z,z^{-1}])^{+}
by Proposition
7.5
. The isomorphism is given by the identification
\alpha_{i}
with
E_{i,i+1}
for
i\neq 0
and
\alpha_{0}
with
zE_{K+1,1}
. Thus after this identification, equation (
50
) implies that

[\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)},E_{i,i+1}]=zE_{i,i+1},\ [\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)},zE_{K+1,1}]=z^{2}E_{K+1,1}

But then one can write
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}
as linear combination of commutators of
E_{i,i+1}
and
zE_{K+1}
. Thus if
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\neq 0
then
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}]\neq 0
. While for
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,-2}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
,
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}]\neq 0
is Proposition
16.2
. Since
\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)}
is an element of cohomological degree
0
, for any
\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}
,
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)},\alpha_{\mathbf{d}}]\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}+\delta}
. So we have checked that the morphism

[\gamma_{\delta}^{(1)},\penalty-\penalty\hskip 0.0pt]:\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathbf{d}+\delta}

is injective. Since the dimensions are the same, it is an isomorphism.

∎

Proposition 16.4
.

The subspace
\mathfrak{im}_{K}
forms a Lie subalgebra of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\BPS}_{\widetilde{Q^{{K}}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
and is generated by
\gamma_{\delta}^{(r)}
for
r\geq 0
.

Proof.

The claim about generation follows from the definition of
\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(r)}
and the fact that
u
acts by derivation. It suffices to show that for
k\geq 1,n,m\geq 0
, the Lie bracket
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(m)}]\in\mathfrak{im}_{K}
. Since for all
r\geq 1
,
\gamma_{r\cdot\delta}^{(n)}
is of cohomological degree
2n-2
, there must exist
\lambda^{n,m}_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\mu^{n,m}_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that

(51)

[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(m)}]=\lambda^{n,m}_{k}\gamma_{(1+k)\cdot\delta}^{(n+m-1)}+\mu_{k}^{n,m}\beta^{(n+m-2)}_{(1+k)\cdot\delta}

where
\beta_{(1+k)\cdot\delta}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\BPS,0}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
. It suffices to show that
\mu^{n,m}_{k}=0
. Note that showing this for
m=0,n\geq 0
, i.e. to show that
\mu^{n,0}_{k}=0
for all
n\geq 0
is enough, since if for some
i\geq 0
,
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(i)}]\in\mathfrak{im}_{K}
then applying
u
gives that
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n+1)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(i)}]+[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(i+1)}]\in\mathfrak{im}_{K}
. So if
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n+1)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(i)}]\in\mathfrak{im}_{K}
then so does
[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(i+1)}]
. We consider the action
\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W}}
on
V_{K}
as constructed in Proposition
14.14
. We then have

Claim:
For any
m\geq 1
, there exist a non-zero scalar
\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{m}\in\mathbb{Q}
, such that for any
v\in V_{K}
, we have the equality

(52)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m\cdot\delta}^{(n)}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v=\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{m}\left((K+1)p_{m}^{n}([S_{K}])v+mn\sum p_{m}^{n-1}(c_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{i}))v\right)

Proof:
We use the same argument as in Proposition
16.2
. We identify the action
\gamma_{\delta}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v
with
p_{1}([S_{K}])v
, and then the result follows from the repetitive use of formulas in Proposition
14.8
applied to the definition of
\gamma_{m\cdot\delta}^{(n)}
(equation
48
).

\square

It follows from the above claim (
52
), equation (
51
), and Proposition
14.8
, that up to a factor of a non-zero scalar, for all
v\in V_{K}
, we have

[\gamma_{\delta}^{(n)},\gamma_{k\cdot\delta}^{(0)}]\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v=\gamma_{(1+k)\cdot\delta}^{(n-1)}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v

To connlude, tt suffices to show that for some
v\in V_{K}
,
u^{n-2}\alpha_{(1+k)\cdot\delta}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v\neq 0
. By Proposition
14.15
, the action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\Im}_{\widetilde{Q^{K}},\widetilde{W^{K}}}
on
V_{K}
is faithful, thus the statement that there exists
v\in V_{K}
such that
u^{n-2}\alpha_{(1+k)\cdot\delta}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v\neq 0
is true for
n=2
. Now, assume otherwise for some
n>2
. Then for all
v
,
[\gamma_{\delta}^{0},u^{n-2}\alpha_{n\cdot\delta}]\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\kern 0.55556pt\rule[0.55556pt]{3.88889pt}{3.88889pt}\kern 0.55556pt}}v=0
. But by Proposition
16.3.1
,
[\gamma_{\delta},u^{n-2}\alpha_{k\cdot\delta}]=u^{n-3}\alpha^{\prime}_{(k+1)\cdot\delta}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}_{(k+1)\cdot\delta}
. Thus action of
u^{n-3}\alpha^{\prime}_{(k+1)\cdot\delta}
isn’t faithful either. Thus, we inductively conclude that action of some non-zero
